# Changelog

Notable changes to the dusk compiler, the standard library, and the dawn package tool. Each entry matches a tagged release, newest first. Commit messages carry the highlights and this file carries the detail.

## 1.16.0

The counted loop. Counting from one number to another is the loop a program writes more often than any other and the one dusk made the most work of, a `mut` counter, a `while` against a bound, a step written into the body, and a mutable binding that outlives the loop that needed it, four lines and a lasting piece of state to say count from here to there. This release gives `for` the count directly. `for i in lo..hi` walks the half open range a slice index already spells, `for i in lo..hi by step` moves the binder by a literal step in either direction, and `by` is a contextual word rather than a reserved one, so no name any existing program spells is taken away from it. The loop is not a builtin and code generation learns nothing: the checker classifies the head and the same pass that has rewritten a container loop since 1.11.0 rewrites this one, before monomorphization, into the `while` a program would have written by hand, with the binder a fresh immutable `int64` on every iteration rather than a `mut` anybody can reach afterward. It is a widening only surface change, a head that was a parse error before is a loop now and nothing that compiled stops compiling, and the counted loop appears nowhere in the toolchain's own source, so the previous release builds this source unchanged. Beside it a sweep folds 142 nested `if` statements in that source into the `&&` and the `else if` they were already saying.

The loop.

- `for i in lo..hi { ... }` binds `lo`, then `lo + 1`, and on up to but not including `hi`, the same half open reading `xs[lo..hi]` takes, so `for i in 0..xs.len` walks exactly the indices `xs[0..xs.len]` views and one meaning of `..` covers the loop head and the slice index both. A `hi` at or below `lo` runs the body zero times rather than faulting or wrapping around. Both bounds are evaluated once, at loop entry and left before right, so a call in a bound runs one time however many iterations follow it and assigning to a name a bound read does not move a trip count the loop has already fixed.
- `for i in lo..hi by step` moves the binder by `step` where the plain form moves it by one. The step is an integer literal and never zero: a positive step counts up and the loop runs while the binder is below `hi`, a negative step counts down and the loop runs while the binder is above `hi`, so a descending loop is written with `lo` above `hi`, `for i in 10..0 by -2` giving `10 8 6 4 2`, and `for i in 0..10 by -1` runs the body zero times instead of forever. The sign of the literal is what picks the comparison the rewritten loop is built around, which is why the step is a literal rather than an expression: a step that is not one is refused, `the step after 'by' must be an integer literal, as in for i in 0..n by 2`, and `by 0` is refused with it, `a step of 0 never advances the loop; use a nonzero step`.
- `by` is read as a keyword in exactly one place, a `for` head that has just finished reading a range. Everywhere else it is an ordinary identifier, so a binding, a parameter, a struct field, a function, and a method each named `by` keep their spelling, and the release reserves no token at all. A contextual word costs the parser one lookahead at one position and costs no program a name, which is the same trade `collector` took when it landed. In a `for` head over anything but a range it is named rather than read as an identifier, `'by' steps a counted range; write for i in lo..hi by 2`, since a step has nothing to say to a container, an array, a slice, or a string.
- A counted head is classified by its syntax rather than by a type. The parser knows the loop is a count the moment it reads the `..`, so the type based classification an array, a slice, a string, a `*Vector`, and a `*Map` go through never sees a range and the `cannot iterate <type>` message keeps the list it has carried. The two binder form stays the map's own: `for i, j in 0..n` is refused, `two loop names bind a map's key and value; a range yields one integer`.
- A bound is `int64` and only `int64`. A bound that is not an integer at all is refused naming what it found, `a range bound must be an integer, not float64`, a range nested as another range's bound included, `a range bound must be an integer, not a range`; an integer of another width or another signedness is refused too, `a range bound must be int64, not uint32; cast the bound, as in int64(n)`, with the cast in the message the whole of the fix; a `char` and a `rune`, which stand in for an integer in plenty of other positions, answer to that same cast message here, `a range bound must be int64, not char; cast the bound, as in int64(n)`, written directly as `for i in 0..'A'` or laundered in through a generic call alike, since the head and the binding the rewrite produces are judged by one rule and the ground pass backstop therefore names the bound rather than a synthesized name the program never wrote; and a bound whose type is still a bare type parameter inside a generic body is refused rather than left to monomorphization, `a range bound must be int64; a value of type parameter T is not known to be one`, since the rewrite runs before the type is ground. This is stricter on purpose than the slice index range, which widens what it can. A counted loop is rewritten into source the program never wrote, so a conversion inserted there is invisible at the head that asked for it, and a cast between the signed and unsigned families saturates, which would turn a bound into a different number than the one spelled with nothing on the screen to say so. A bare literal bound adapts to `int64` the way a literal adapts at every other binding position, so `for i in 0..10` needs no suffix and no cast.
- The binder is a fresh immutable `int64` on each iteration. Assigning to it is refused by the rule every other binding answers to, `cannot assign to immutable 'i'`, so the loop's own step is the only thing that moves the count and a body wanting a different number computes one into a binding of its own; there is no annotation on the binder and its type is always `int64`. `break` and `continue` mean what they mean in every other loop. The step runs ahead of the body, so a `continue`, which jumps to the loop's condition, lands on a count that has already advanced and a loop that skips terminates instead of spinning on the same number.
- The rewrite is the one 1.11.0 built. A classified counted loop becomes, before monomorphization, a hidden counter `__ri$N` initialized to `lo` and a hidden bound `__rhi$N` holding the once evaluated `hi`, both annotated `int64` so the ground pass that follows monomorphization checks them against a declared type instead of inferring one, then a comparison of the counter against the bound in the direction the step's sign chose, the binder bound off the counter, the step, and the body, all of it spanned to the `for` header. The rewrite first appears in the `mono` dump and the `parse` and `desugar` dumps carry the loop exactly as written. Inside an async body the counter and the bound are task frame slots the way a container loop's are, so a loop that awaits in its body resumes with its count intact.
- Four shapes the form does not have, each on purpose. There is no inclusive counted loop: `..=` stays a slice index form and an inclusive range in a head is refused with the rewrite spelled out, `a counted loop takes a half open range; write lo..hi + 1 instead of lo..=hi`, since two range spellings in a loop head would differ by one character and by one last iteration. The binder takes no annotation. The step is a literal rather than a computed value, the one restriction a later release can lift if a program asks, at the cost of either a direction test run every iteration or a comparison picked at entry and carried in a slot. And the counter wraps at the edge of `int64` like any other `int64` arithmetic in dusk, with no guard of its own: a count carried past the maximum wraps to the minimum and keeps going, exactly what the hand written `while` it replaces did, since a rewrite should not add a check the program would not have had.

The sweep.

- 142 places in the toolchain's own source now say what they meant, 127 folds of an `if` whose block holds nothing but another `if` into one `if` over `&&`, and 15 collapses of an `else` whose block holds nothing but an `if` into an `else if`, across 65 files. The nesting was never a structure, it was a conjunction and a chain written the long way, and reading it cost a reader a block boundary each time to learn that the outer branch had nothing else to do.
- Behavior is identical at every one of them, including the order and the count of the evaluations. `&&` has short circuited since 1.2.0, so the inner condition of a folded pair is evaluated exactly when the outer one was true, once, in the same order the nesting gave it, and an `else if` is already what an `else` holding a lone `if` parses into a branch of. No condition with a side effect changes how often it runs.
- The compiler's own module is the one thing whose IR moves. The fold is a change to a program this tree compiles, not a change to what the compiler does with a program, so every program outside the toolchain lowers to the bytes it lowered to before and the stage ladder re-fixes on new hashes because the source it is building is different, not because the compiler building it decided anything differently.

The notes.

- The counted loop is spelled nowhere in `compiler/`, `lib/std`, or the tools written in dusk. The ratchet rule this tree holds at every release is that the source never uses a feature the release introduces, so the previous release's binary builds this source with nothing this release added; adopting the form across the toolchain belongs to a release after the one that ships it, where the adoption is the change being proved rather than a passenger on the feature.
- The step is spelled with a word rather than a third range operator. A range already carries two spellings, `..` and `..=`, and a third would be another line of code read off a single character, where `by` is a word a reader cannot mistake for either of the other two and a word the grammar can take back the moment the head ends.
- A fresh binder each iteration is not a fresh capture each iteration, and the spec now says so where the capture rule lives rather than leaving a reader to infer it from the loop. A synchronous lambda's environment is one piece of storage per lambda site, so a closure built inside any loop body and stored past the loop reads the last iteration's captures, the counted loop included and nothing about it new: a `while` and a `for` over an array or a container have always worked this way. A lambda called within its own iteration reads that iteration's values, which is the case a body actually writes, and a lambda inside an `async func` builds a new environment each time and keeps its own.
- The dump allowance is what it has been. `tools/dump-allow-1.16.0.txt` carries the version sentence in the two `@dusk` floor fixtures, which print the running toolchain version and therefore differ across any release pair, and it carries nothing else the previous release and this one disagree about except the examples spelling a form the previous release's parser cannot read.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 1308 records, 29 new since 1.15.1 and every one of them the counted loop: sixteen that run, twelve that must fail to check, and one parse dump pinning the head's own shape before any rewrite touches it. The running ones cover an ascending count, an empty range, a descending count with a negative step, a bound that is a call evaluated once, `break` and `continue` inside a count, a counted loop around an `await`, a counted loop nested in another, and `by` used as an ordinary name in the same file as a counted loop. The failing twelve are a refusal apiece for a zero step, a step that is not a literal, a `by` after an iterand that is not a range, an inclusive range in a head, two binders over a range, a float bound, a range nested as a bound, a bound of another width or signedness, a `char` bound written directly, a `char` bound arriving through a generic call, a bound still typed as a bare type parameter, and an assignment to the binder.
- `tools/dump-differential.sh` compares the `v1.15.1` release binary against this release's over 1324 source files and thirteen commands each, 16978 comparisons: 16793 byte identical, 185 named by `tools/dump-allow-1.16.0.txt`, and zero differences past them. Sixteen of the allowed are the version sentence in the two `@dusk` floor fixtures, `package.dawn asks for dusk 9.9, this is 1.15.1` against `this is 1.16.0`, across the eight commands that reach the loader, the same pair every release before this one allowed. The other 169 are the 28 new examples that spell a counted loop, across the commands that reach a head the previous release cannot parse at all.
- `tools/runner-differential.sh` reports no difference. The runner gains no mode this release, so the `v1.15.1` runner and the runner built from this tree read the same manifest to the same report byte for byte.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.15.1` release binary as seed. stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256, `08b18f031a60bcb1e28b8073a48aa69aa4b96ab80a0a0f936012226857990837`, and one compiler IR sha256, `66e4000d2a57375b8d708bf755e2f6565c3ac620264ecc2380754dc5dc2d3732`, every stage built at `-O2` by a compiler that has `--release` and passes it, the seed included this time, and the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks are green with 1308 of 1308 under both of the ladder's own suite runs. Both hashes are new against 1.15.1's, which is the sweep landing in the compiler's own source and nothing else.
- The ratchet holds. The `v1.15.1` release binary builds the 1.16.0 compiler and the package tool, and the pair that comes out passes the full suite. dawn's offline smoke is green, 105 of 105 checks. The comment differential holds, 1121 byte equal comparisons over 1215 files and zero failures.

## 1.15.1

The performance. Every binary this toolchain has ever produced came out of `clang` with no optimization flag at all, since the link line was written during the bootstrap to mirror the seed compiler's and nothing ever added one, and the program that pays most for that is the compiler itself, the largest dusk program there is and the one that compiles itself over and over at every gate. This release adds the flag that turns optimization on, `dusk build --release`, leaves the default exactly where it was, stops recompiling the C runtime on every single link, replaces the linear free list underneath every dusk program with a size classed one, and spends the difference on the toolchain's own builds. Nothing here changes what a program means. The IR the compiler emits is unchanged under the flag and without it, which is the whole shape of the change: optimization is what `clang` does with the IR, never what the compiler writes, so the same proof that carried the adoption release carries this one, every dump byte identical against the previous release except where this release means to differ. The flag also uncovered a soundness bug older than itself, a collector root scan that read the top of the stack out of a slot the optimizer is free to move, and the fix for that ships here beside it. Two things beside it are behavior changes and are named as such: the quoted git import, promised for removal since 1.14.0 and deferred out of 1.15.0 because that release changed nothing, is gone, and the runtime objects a link consumes are now cached rather than rebuilt.

The flag.

- `dusk build --release <file>` compiles the emitted `.ll`, the seven C sources of the runtime, and every `@csource` at `-O2`, where a plain build compiles all three at `-O0`. The flag reaches `clang` and stops there. The pipeline ahead of it is untouched, so `dusk ir` prints the same bytes either way, `dusk check` reaches the same verdict, and a release build and a plain build of one program differ in the machine code and in nothing the compiler itself decided.
- The default does not move. A `dusk build` with no flag, and `dusk run`, which takes none at all, are `-O0` exactly as they were, since the build you take while writing code should be quick to produce and honest in a debugger, and the optimized build is the one you ask for by name.
- `--release` composes with `--lib`. `dusk build --lib --release <file>` writes the same `lib<stem>.a` and `<stem>.h` pair the plain library build writes, with the module object, the runtime objects, and every `@csource` object inside the archive built at `-O2`. The header is byte identical either way, since a prototype is a fact about the ABI and not about the optimizer.
- `dawn build --release` passes the flag straight through to the compiler, so a package gets the same choice with the same spelling. Neither tool's `run` takes it. `dusk run --release <file>` is refused by name, `dusk: run takes no --release; build --release <file>, then run target/dusk-out/<stem>`, which is the two commands the one flag would have folded into, and `dawn run` keeps handing every word after it to the program's own argv exactly as it always has, since a flag read there would quietly take a word out of a program's arguments. An optimized binary is a thing you build once and run many times, so the flag lives where the building is.
- An unrecognized flag on `build` is named rather than read as a path: `dusk: unknown build flag '--relase'; build takes --release and --lib`, so a typo costs a sentence instead of a failed open on a file nobody meant to name. The usage the CLI prints carries the new line beside the old ones, `build --release <file>  the same build at -O2, slower to link and faster to run`, and dawn's usage carries `build [--release]`.
- The trade is the ordinary one and both halves of it are worth writing down. `clang` at `-O2` over the compiler's own 12MB of IR takes 16s against 1.8s at `-O0`, so a release build of the compiler costs about fourteen seconds more than a plain one. The compiler that comes out, measured with the runtime and the compiler as they stood before the work below, runs its own `check` over the compiler root in 1.27s against 2.6s and emits its own IR in 4.2s against 10.0s, a bit over twice as fast on identical input, and the binary is 3.22MB against 3.85MB. You pay once where the build happens and collect on every run after it, which is why the toolchain's own builds take the flag and a build you are iterating on does not.
- The scripts that stand the toolchain up build it with `--release` from here: the bootstrap's rebuild from source, the stage ladder's own stages, and the AUR packaging's self build each pass the flag when the compiler doing the building is 1.15.1 or newer, and skip it when it is older, since a compiler that predates the flag would refuse an argument it has never heard of. `tools/ratchet.sh` never passes it. The ratchet exists to prove that the previous release's binary builds this source with nothing this release added, and a flag this release added is exactly the thing it must not need.

The cache.

- A link used to hand `clang` the seven runtime C sources as source, every time, so every `dusk build`, every `dusk run`, and every one of the suite's records compiled the same runtime from scratch before it could link. The objects are cached now. The first link in a tree compiles them and every link after it reuses what is on disk, and the runtime compile, which was a fixed cost paid once per program built, becomes a fixed cost paid once per tree.
- An object lands beside the build's other output, `target/dusk-out/runtime-<source>-<key>.o` for a compiler build and the same spelling under `target/dawn-out/` for a package build, one object per runtime source and seven of them in a full link. The key is 16 hex digits over three things: the first line of `clang --version`, the optimization level the object was compiled at, and a hash of the runtime's own text, every runtime header's name, length, and bytes folded into every key and each source's own name, length, and bytes folded into its own. Editing a header therefore moves all seven keys, since a header reaches every object, and editing one source moves exactly that source's key and leaves the other six alone. A `clang` upgrade and a switch between a plain and a release build each miss the same way, so a plain build and a release build in one tree keep two full sets side by side and neither invalidates the other.
- An entry is written to a temporary name and renamed into place, so a reader either sees a whole object or sees nothing and rebuilds it. Two builds racing in one tree cannot hand each other a half written file, and a build killed partway leaves the cache exactly as it found it.
- A cache that will not work is never a build that fails. Every step of it falls back to handing `clang` the source the way the link always did: a directory that cannot be made, an object that cannot be written, a rename that does not land, a cached object that cannot be read. The cache can make a build faster and it has no path by which it can make one fail, which is the only property worth having in something that sits between every program and its link line.
- There is no eviction and no expiry, and there is no command to prune one. The cache is entirely inside `target/`, so `rm -rf target/` clears it along with everything else the build wrote, which is the same thing that clears a stale binary today and is the only thing anyone needs to remember about it.
- A hello program cost 0.30s to build before this release. It costs 0.39s on the cold build that fills the cache and 0.12s on every warm build after it, so the second build of anything is a bit over twice as fast as the first build ever was, and the first is slower by the write. Under `--release` the same program is 0.81s cold and 0.26s warm, where the optimization is the whole of the remaining cost. The suite feels it hardest, since it builds and links 1279 records in one run and every one of them used to compile the runtime first: a full run takes about 70 seconds now against about twenty minutes, the cache carrying most of that and the faster compiler the rest.

The runtime.

- The collector's root scan finds the top of the stack by asking the platform for it. It used to take the address of the first `alloca` in the emitted `main` as the upper bound of the region it walks, and to spill the registers by writing a `setjmp` `jmp_buf` into that region. Both hold at `-O0` and neither is guaranteed: the optimizer is free to place that slot below every other local `main` holds, which puts `main`'s own collected roots above the bound where the scan never reads them, and glibc mangles the frame pointer it stores in a `jmp_buf` through `PTR_MANGLE`, so a root live only in `rbp` is not a pointer any scan can recognize. A live block reachable from `main` could therefore be swept, and what the program then read was memory the collector had already reclaimed. Nothing about this is new, it has been true of the scan since the collector shipped, and it was unreachable in practice because every build this toolchain produced was `-O0`. `--release` is what makes it reachable, so the flag and the fix land together.
- The anchor now records the real base. `cool_gc_anchor` asks the calling thread for its own mapped stack region, `pthread_getattr_np` with `pthread_attr_getstack`, and falls back to `__libc_stack_end`, the stack pointer the process entered with, declared weak so a C library without it reads as absent rather than failing the link. Either answer is taken only when it sits above the address `main` handed in and within a gigabyte of it, since a bound below `main`'s frame would be worse than the one it replaces and a bound implausibly far above it is not this stack at all; where neither answer qualifies, the handed in address stands and the scan is exactly what it was. The register spill is `__builtin_unwind_init()`, which forces the callee saved registers into the frame being scanned rather than into a structure the C library is entitled to obfuscate.
- The fix is proved at both link modes rather than argued. `examples/gcrelease_ok.dusk` holds a collected root in `main` across a forced collection and is an ordinary `run` record; `release_collector` is a special that builds the same file with `build --release`, runs it, and requires the same `720\n9\n` the plain build prints, which is the one check in the suite that can see a root the optimizer moved. Beside it, all 22 collector examples produce byte identical output plain and under `--release`. The IR is unchanged by the whole of it, since every line of this is C in the runtime.
- The managed heap files a freed block by its size. A `free` used to push the block onto one list that every later `alloc` walked looking for something big enough, so a program that frees a lot pays a scan on every allocation after it, and the compiler, which allocates constantly and frees constantly, pays it hardest. A block now goes on the list for its exact payload size, a direct table covering every size from 0 through 4096 bytes and an open addressing table for everything above that, so parking a block and taking one back are each a constant time operation and a reuse is an exact fit rather than the first block that happened to be large enough. The list nodes live in arrays of their own rather than threaded through the payloads, so parking a block never writes into memory something else may still be reading.
- The double free guard is exact now, where it used to be a window. A parked block is marked in bit 62 of its own header size word, set when the block is freed and cleared when it is handed back out, so `fatal: double free` and the 134 it exits with cover every parked block on the heap. The old guard remembered a fixed 4096 parked blocks and compared a free against those, which meant a second free of anything past that window was not caught at all: it went through silently and corrupted the free list instead of aborting. The bit costs a ceiling on one allocation, 2^62 bytes, which is not a size any machine this compiler runs on can ask for. The 4096 cap goes with it, and it cost nothing to remove, since exceeding it never returned memory to libc and only gave up the chance to reuse a block.
- The allocation registry is append only. A block's address is never handed back to libc and a reuse is an exact size match, so one entry describes one block for the life of the process and no entry ever has to be found and rewritten. Whether a block is live is read from its parked bit, and the snapshot the collector walks for roots filters on that bit rather than on a second structure someone has to keep in step with the first. The debug allocator is untouched by this and keeps the table it has always kept of its own.
- `cool_str_eq` answers a pointer comparison first, so two strings at one address are equal without reading a byte. That is the common case everywhere the compiler compares an interned name, which it does on nearly every name it resolves.
- Two standard library shapes get the same treatment. A `StringBuilder` push measures its argument once, grows once, and copies once, where it walked the argument several times on the way through; the capacity sequence is untouched, so a builder grows at exactly the sizes it grew at before. The generic map picks a slot by masking the hash rather than dividing by the capacity, which the table's own invariant already permitted, since a map's capacity is a power of two at every size it takes.
- The measurement, one IR in and the same IR out, the runtime as it was against the runtime as it is: the compiler emits its own IR in 1.06s against 4.27s at `-O2` and in 2.44s against 9.18s at `-O0`, and checks its own root in 0.74s against 1.17s. Peak resident memory is 890MB against 893MB, so none of it trades memory for time. The IR is byte identical across the change, which is what makes this a measurement of one program rather than a comparison of two.

The compiler.

- The escape summary stops rebuilding a string to add to it. A function's callers were kept as one newline joined string, so every edge added to the graph rebuilt the whole string, and a function with many callers cost time quadratic in the number of edges reaching it. The callers are a `Map<string, *Vector<string>>` now and an edge is an append. The worklist visits them in the order it always did, so the summaries that come out are the summaries that came out before, reached by an operation that does not grow with what is already there.
- The C type arena looks a named type up rather than scanning for it. Resolving a name walked the whole item table on every question, so the cost of one lookup grew with the size of the program being compiled and the compiler, which is the largest program this tree compiles, asked the question constantly. The table is built once, name to item with the first spelling of a name winning exactly as the scan's first hit did, and a lookup is a single probe.
- Interning stops asking the same question twice. `ty_canon` answers with the constant for a leaf type and for each of the eight integer spellings directly instead of assembling one through the builder, and `ty_intern`, `cty_intern_full`, `intern_id`, and `intern_owned` each replace a `map_has` followed by a `map_get` with the one probe that answers both. A hash and a probe are cheap on their own; the compiler asks for them often enough that asking twice was worth stopping.
- The measurement, one source at `-O0` over the runtime this release already fixed: `check compiler/dusk.dusk` in 1.23s against 1.55s, `ir` of the same root in 2.01s against 2.62s, and `esc` in 1.09s against 1.34s, a fifth off each. Peak resident memory does not move, since none of this trades space for time; it stops doing work that was never necessary.
- Every change in this group is behavior preserving and proved so the same way the adoption release proved its rewrite, by requiring every dump of every command to be byte identical, here between this compiler before the change and after it, 16588 comparisons with zero differences. A performance change to a compiler is a change nobody asked to see in the output, so the gate is the one that would catch it if it were.
- 22 functions come out. Ten are called from nowhere, left behind by earlier splits the way the 22 the adoption release deleted were. The other twelve are duplicates, one implementation of a thing kept in two places, folded into the copy that stays across six groups: `astdump`'s seven renderers, `render_type`, `render_type_list`, `render_param`, `render_param_list`, `render_bind`, `render_bind_list`, and `render_kid_string_list`, fold into `astdumpty`; `dir_of` into `loaddirs`; `gm_error_string` into `gen_error_string`; `push_hex_min` into `dumpval`; `fscall_extract` into `genprint`'s `gp_extract_part`; and `no_variant_msg` into `tcmatch`. A second copy of a function is worse than a long one, since a fix reaches one of them and the other keeps answering the old way.
- Two struct fields nothing reads are gone, `ImportRes.target` and `VariantSig.owner`, and one guard that could not be false, `has_mod_ns`, goes with them. A field nobody reads still has to be written correctly by everyone who builds the struct, and a condition that is always true reads like a case that exists.
- 127 dead import lines are gone, 112 in the compiler and 15 in the runner. 26 of them are `@import std.io` in a file that prints, which needs no import at all, since `print` and `printerr` are builtins rather than library functions; with those removed, `io.dusk` is out of the toolchain's import graph entirely. The import closure of every program in the tree, the set of files and the order they load in, is unchanged by the sweep, so no file's privatize suffix moves and nothing about what a name resolves to shifts underneath the removal.
- 116 `export` markers come off names only their own file uses. An export marker is a claim that a name is part of a file's surface, and a name nothing outside the file spells is not; leaving the marker on tells the loader to keep the name unrenamed, so it also puts a name in the flat module that nothing wanted there. A name that stops being exported is renamed with its file's own suffix, which moves symbols inside the compiler's own IR and moves nothing about what the compiler compiles, since a program's symbols come from that program's files.
- The compiler's own module emits 2935 defines against 2961, which is the whole census arriving in the output. What the compiler does with a program is unchanged, proved by the differential rather than by the count. Three imports that looked dead are still standing: `tcexpr`'s `tcesc3` turned out to be load bearing for the load graph rather than for `tcexpr` itself, so the four files that actually use it import it themselves now and the graph is unchanged; `std.string` in `lib/std/map.dusk` and the allocator import in the arena are left where they are, since these are standard library files where an import is part of what a program reaching the module gets, and that is a judgment for a release that makes it deliberately rather than for a sweep.

The import.

- The quoted git import is removed. Before 1.14.0 a dependency was named at the import itself, `@import "github.com/user/repo/module"`, resolved against a global clone cache with no reference and no lock behind it; 1.14.0 refused the form inside a project and left it resolving outside one for a release, and 1.15.0 carried it one release further because that release had promised to change nothing. It resolves nowhere now. Any import whose value carries a `/` is refused wherever it is written, inside a project and outside one alike, `'example.com/user/repo/mod' is a url import; declare it with @require in package.dawn`, which is the sentence 1.14.0 already printed inside a project and is now simply the answer everywhere. The form outside a project earns a record of its own, a file with no manifest anywhere above it refused by that same sentence rather than by a generic unresolved import, since the point of the removal is that the answer no longer depends on whether a manifest happens to sit over the file.
- The loader no longer reads `DAWN_CACHE`. The old resolution walked that root looking for a checkout to compile against, which meant an import could reach a directory no lock stood behind and no manifest named, and the whole path is gone rather than left dormant. dawn keeps its own use of the variable, the bare mirror cache a fetch clones out of, which is a fetch time optimization that never resolves an import; the two shared a name and were never the same thing.
- Moving a program off the form is two steps and neither of them is new: write a `@require` for the repository at the tag you want, and spell the import through the alias the require declared. A project already on the manifest never wrote the old form to begin with, since it has been refused there for two releases.

The notes.

- The resource rule this tree works under was measured for a compiler that no longer exists. A self build peaks at 0.86GB and finishes in 11.8s on this machine, where the number written down dated from the 0.9.x era and read eleven gigabytes, so the cage the scripts put around a build stays as a habit and its numbers stop describing a limit anybody is near. The hour a release gate takes is in the suite and the stage ladder, which build the compiler's roots many times over, not in any one build.
- The IR is untouched by every optimization in this release, which is a claim with a proof rather than an intention. `tools/dump-differential.sh` runs the previous release's binary and this one's over the same tree and requires all thirteen dump commands, stdout, stderr, and exit code, to match byte for byte, so a hotspot fix that changed what the compiler emits fails the gate before it reaches a golden.
- The caller index the escape summary builds is not torn down when the process ends. Emptying a `Map` one key at a time through `map_take` is quadratic in the entry count, so a teardown would cost more at exit than the index saved during the walk, and the memory goes back to the operating system at exit either way. The right fix is an operation in `std.map` that forgets an entry's insertion order in constant time, which would make the teardown linear, and it belongs to the release that adds it rather than to this one.
- The append only registry moves one cost the other way, and it is worth naming rather than leaving for someone to find. The snapshot the collector takes for its root scan used to be proportional to the live block count and is now proportional to every block the process has ever minted, since an entry stays and the parked bit is what filters it. A program that mints heavily, collects, and then keeps running pays a little more per collection for an allocator that is faster on every `alloc` and `free` between collections, and the tradeoff is the right one for the programs this toolchain runs, which allocate constantly and collect rarely.
- The cache key covers the runtime's own text and nothing outside the tree. A system header that changed under one `clang` version, or an include path the environment moved, is invisible to it, since hashing the world a compiler might read is a cost every link would pay to catch a case a developer creates on purpose. `rm -rf target/` is the reset, and it is the same reset that clears a stale binary.
- The wasm target is unchanged. `dusk ir --target=wasm32 <file>` cross-emits the same module it did, since it emits IR and never invokes `clang`, and `--release` has nothing to say to a command that does not link.
- The stage ladder no longer collapses on one binary hash, and where it splits says exactly which stage was built by what. The seed is the previous release's binary, which has no `--release` to pass, so stage1 comes out at `-O0` while stage1 and stage2, both 1.15.1, build the stages after them at `-O2`. stage2 and stage3 share one binary sha256 and stage1 stands apart from them, which is the arithmetic of the flag rather than a compiler disagreeing with itself: one program compiled at two optimization levels is two files. The compiler IR sha256 is the same across all three, as it always was, and that is the hash the fixpoint is read on, since the IR is what the compiler decided and the binary is what `clang` did with it.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 1279 records, 8 new since 1.15.0 and every one of them covering something this release added or fixed: a url import outside a project refused by name, four for the flag, a `--release` build printing what the plain build prints, `run` refusing the flag, an unknown build flag named rather than opened as a path, and a `--release --lib` archive, a raw double free aborting instead of corrupting the free list, and two for the collector root, the plain run and the `release_collector` special that builds the same file at `-O2`. The run takes about 70 seconds where it took about twenty minutes, which is the runtime object cache and the faster compiler landing in the one place that builds and links more programs than anything else here.
- `tools/dump-differential.sh` compares the `v1.15.0` release binary against this release's over 1296 source files and thirteen commands each, 16614 comparisons: 16590 byte identical, 24 named by `tools/dump-allow-1.15.1.txt`, and zero differences past them. The 24 are the two changes this release makes on purpose and nothing else. Sixteen are the version sentence in the two `@dusk` floor fixtures, `package.dawn asks for dusk 9.9, this is 1.15.0` against `this is 1.15.1`, across the eight commands that reach the loader. The other eight are `examples/urlimport_noproject_fail.dusk`, which the previous release resolved against a cache and this one refuses, across those same eight.
- `tools/runner-differential.sh` reports one difference and it is the one a reader can predict. The `v1.15.0` runner does not know the `release_collector` special this release adds, so it reports 1278 of 1279 where the runner built from this tree reports 1279, and every other line of the two reports matches byte for byte. A runner cannot execute a mode it has never heard of; what the gate proves is that the single difference is that record and that nothing else moved beside it.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.15.0` release binary as seed. stage2 and stage3 share one binary sha256, `bf0e41c62dd122ab26aa7a28c574486e1a6737c4345635e7f5d52bde0c228367`, both built at `-O2` by a compiler that has the flag; stage1 is the same program at `-O0`, `72e6eec6`, since the seed predates the flag and passes none. All three share one compiler IR sha256, `ad88531904865f7f840ac5c8df76983dbf9bbec93da19bb48dc2e72e4085f934`, which is the hash the fixpoint is read on, and the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks are green with 1279 of 1279 under both of the ladder's own suite runs.
- The ratchet holds in both halves. The `v1.15.0` release binary builds the 1.15.1 compiler and the package tool with no flag of its own, and the pair that comes out rebuilds itself with `--release`, so the previous release needs nothing this release added and this release's own build takes what it added. dawn's offline smoke is green, 105 of 105 checks. The comment differential holds, 1091 byte equal comparisons over 1187 files and zero failures.
- The shipped release binary is 2.48MB and checks the compiler root in 0.59s, where the `v1.15.0` binary took 2.4s on the same source, about four times faster with the flag, the allocator, and the compiler's own hot paths each taking a share of it. The runtime object cache beside it: a hello build 0.39s cold and 0.12s warm against the 0.30s it cost before, and 0.81s cold and 0.26s warm under `--release`.
## 1.15.0

The adoption. Seven releases put spellings into the language that this tree was not allowed to write, since the ratchet rule holds every file here to the surface the previous release already had, so `++`, `+=` where it was spelled long, `for x in v`, the `if` and `match` expressions, and `f"..."` all landed in the language and none of them landed in the compiler. This release pays that debt in one pass: the compiler's 136 files, the standard library, the test runner, and dawn are rewritten in the idiom the language grew between 1.8.0 and 1.14.1. Nothing about the language moves. There is no new syntax, no changed rule, no new or reworded diagnostic, and no changed byte in the output of any command past the compiler's own version number, which two package fixtures print inside a refusal and which moves at every release. That is the entire claim of the release and the reason it comes with a proof built for it: `tools/dump-differential.sh` runs the previous release's binary and this one's over the same tree and requires every dump the pipeline can print, and every exit code beside it, to be byte identical across all thirteen commands. An adoption release is exactly the release where a behavior change would be an accident, so the gate is written to catch one rather than to be argued about.

The sweep.

- 1295 `x = x + 1` and `x = x - 1` statements come out of the compiler, the standard library, the runner, and dawn. 1154 of them become `x++` and `x--`, each one in the statement position the long form already sat in, so the value the expression form yields is read nowhere and the rewrite is the same step written shorter. The other 141 become nothing at all: 140 are the index bump at the tail of a loop the container form absorbed, gone with the loop that held them, and one stood in code this sweep deleted outright.
- `x = x + e` becomes `x += e`, 110 sites, kept to the case where the assignment target and the left operand are the same name spelled the same way, so a compound form never has to be read twice to see what it accumulates into.
- The canonical index loop becomes `for x in v`, 140 sites, 135 in the compiler, 1 in the standard library, and 4 in the runner. The compiler's share is spread across the whole pipeline rather than pooled in one pass, which is the sign that the shape was the tree's habit and not one file's. The rewrite is taken only where the loop is exactly a walk: an index starting at 0, a bound that is the container's own length, an increment at the tail of the body, and a body that spells the index nowhere but in the element read. A loop that uses its index for anything else, a lookahead, a second container, a bound it edits, keeps the shape it had, since the point is to say walk where the code walks and not to bury an index that is doing work.
- The two arm and match shapes turned up no site that met the purity rule, so none moved. The rewrite is only sound where every arm is a plain value assigned to one binding, and each candidate here failed that on one of two counts: an arm calls a function, which the expression form would move relative to the arm's other work, or the `if` has no `else`, which leaves a path where the binding takes no value at all. Both spellings stay in the language and this tree simply had no place for them; a later change that opens one of those arms up may well earn a rewrite the sweep could not.
- `mut` comes off 5 bindings that never needed it, two in the parser and three in code generation. It is a small count for a real point: a binding written once is a stronger statement than a binding the checker merely observes is written once, and a compiler file that declares `mut` only where a value really moves says which of its own locals are settled.
- String building in the runner and dawn becomes `f"..."`, 129 sites, 102 in the runner and 27 in dawn, which is where this tree does most of its message assembly. Interpolation carries a `string` and an `int64` hole, so a site whose pieces are all one of those becomes one literal and a site holding a value of any other type keeps its concatenation and its explicit conversion rather than being half converted into the new form.
- 22 exported functions nothing calls are deleted, 21 in the compiler and one in the runner, left over from earlier splits and rewrites. Every one is an `export func`, so what settles it is not the loader's renaming of private names but a search of the whole tree: the compiler, the standard library, the tests, the tools, the runtime, the docs, the examples, the spec, and the README hold no reference to any of them, and these files are the toolchain's own rather than a surface anything outside the tree imports. They are `push_utf8`, `source_byte`, `source_len`, `intern_len`, `p_ident2_text_is`, `ps_union`, `func_params`, `mt_named_id`, `tuple_kids`, `struct_gens`, `sig_variant`, `typeck_check`, `cty_is_aggregate`, `genctx_nom`, `gen_load`, `gen_val`, `gen_gc_collect`, `gen_gc_collections`, `gen_gc_live_blocks`, `gen_gc_live_bytes`, `build_module_to`, and the runner's `both_streams`.

The proof.

- `tools/dump-differential.sh <old-dusk> <new-dusk> [allow-file] [source-list]` is new and is the release's own gate. It runs two compilers over one source set and requires every observable output to match: `lex`, `scan`, `parse`, `load`, `desugar`, `check`, `check --json`, `mono`, `esc`, `ir`, `ir --target=wasm32`, `doc`, and `doc --json`, with stdout, stderr, and the exit code compared as a single byte stream per command, so a compiler that got the same answer by a different route or with a different fault line still fails. Both binaries read this tree through `DUSK_HOME`, which is what makes the comparison fair: two correct compilers over one library and one runtime must agree on every dump, the compiler's own roots included.
- The default source set is every `.dusk` under `examples/`, 1184 of them, each static package fixture's root under `tests/dawn/`, every standard library module, `tests/runner/testrun.dusk`, and both compiler roots, `compiler/dusk.dusk` and `compiler/dawn.dusk`. A named list file replaces it for a narrower run. Each command runs caged, under an address space limit and a timeout and at low priority, and the whole script runs serially, since a self build class load beside it is what makes a machine swap rather than a compiler disagree.
- The allow list is a file of `<stem> <command>` lines and holds one thing this release, the version number itself. `tests/dawn/dusktoonew` and `tests/dawn/depdusktoonew` each declare `@dusk 9.9` and are refused with the running compiler named in the sentence, `package.dawn asks for dusk 9.9, this is 1.14.1` against `this is 1.15.0`, so the two binaries disagree on the eight commands that reach the loader, `load`, `desugar`, `check`, `check --json`, `mono`, `esc`, `ir`, and `ir --target=wasm32`. `tools/dump-allow-1.15.0.txt` records those 16 pairs and nothing else, which is why the exception is written down rather than the gate turned off around it: a version number printed by a refusal is the one place in this release where a byte was ever going to move.
- The runner is proved the same way it proves everything else, by `tools/runner-differential.sh <prev-tag> <prev-dusk> <dusk-under-test>`, also new. It takes the previous release's runner source out of git at that tag, builds it with the previous release's compiler in a tree of its own, and runs it over the current `tests/goldens.manifest` beside the runner built from this tree, requiring both reports to be byte identical and both to pass. A runner the sweep rewrote could otherwise redefine a pass by rewording a report or by comparing a little differently, and this is the one gate that would see it, since the runner cannot be its own witness.
- The gates that were already here carry the rest. The comment differential still finds the emitted IR unmoved by a comment rewrite, the ratchet runs with the `v1.14.1` release binary as seed, and the stage ladder re-fixes. The ratchet is the constraint that made this release possible only now: every spelling the sweep adopts shipped in 1.14.1 or earlier, so the previous release's binary builds this source, which is the same rule that kept the compiler from spelling any of them at the time each one landed.

The notes.

- The quoted git import survives one more release. 1.14.0 said the form would be removed in 1.15.0, and it is not: this release carries no behavior change of any kind, and removing a way to spell an import is a behavior change, so it moves to 1.15.1 with the migration unchanged, a `@require` for the repository at the tag you want and the import respelled through the alias. The form still resolves only outside a project and still reaches nothing but a cache placed by hand.
- The unsigned integer types are not adopted. A count or an index in this tree is guarded with `x < 0` in a good many places, and under an unsigned type that comparison is statically false, so the sweep would be trading a live guard for a dead one and calling it an improvement. Adopting the widths is a change to what those guards mean and belongs in a release that says so, not in a rewrite that promises to change nothing.
- The statement terminator is not adopted. A `;` at the end of a statement is legal and this tree writes one statement per line, so a terminator adds a character and says nothing the newline did not. It is a style choice and the style here is the one already on the page.
- The owning `take` is not adopted, for the plain reason that no site in this tree wants it. The blessing exists for a caller that must take ownership out of a borrowed place, and the compiler, the standard library, dawn, and the runner all own what they free through the paths they already had.
- Map iteration is not rewritten. A `for k in m` walks the map's live positions, asking `map_len` and `map_key_at` on every turn, while the explicit walks in this tree take a snapshot first, `map_keys(m)` returning a vector the loop then reads whatever the map does afterward. A body that inserts into or removes from the map it is walking sees a different sequence under each, so the two are not the same loop and swapping one for the other is a semantic change wearing a rewrite's clothes. The explicit walks stay.
- A rewrite is only invisible where the dumps cover it, and they cover a great deal: the token stream, the parse, the merged module, the desugared form, the checker's verdict in text and in JSON, the ground module, the escape summary, the IR for both targets, and the extracted documentation in both forms. What no dump reads is what the source looks like, which is the whole point of the release, and the sweep is judged there by reading it.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 1271 records, and the manifest is unchanged at 1271 records, since a rewrite that changes no output adds no golden. An adoption release has no surface to cover, so what a record could prove here is that the rewritten code still answers as it did, which is what the differentials prove over far more programs than a record reaches.
- `tools/dump-differential.sh` compares the `v1.14.1` release binary against this release's over 1275 source files and thirteen commands each, 1184 examples, 73 package fixture roots, 15 standard library modules, the runner's root, and the two compiler roots, for 16575 comparisons: 16559 byte identical, the 16 the allow list names, each of those a refusal sentence carrying the compiler's version, and zero differences past them. `tools/runner-differential.sh` passes beside it, the `v1.14.1` runner and this release's reporting 1271 of 1271 alike over the current manifest.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.14.1` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (`dba96038226cd35ae5f1993a47b0bea5eca3678ad53902d8f6bc60ad5a4b939c`) and one compiler IR sha256 (`95f0cfe21b6f6c848ddefa8a499c712430c8b59670773cd785dcabf490f3c80e`), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 1271 of 1271 under both of the ladder's own suite runs, each stage building the package tool for its own run. The ratchet holds: the `v1.14.1` release binary builds the 1.15.0 compiler and the package tool, and the freshly built pair passes the full suite. dawn's offline smoke is green, 105 of 105 checks. The comment differential holds, 1087 byte equal comparisons over 1184 files and zero failures.

## 1.14.1

The cache and the name. 1.14.0 put the package system in place and this release closes the two holes that using it found. A fetch went to the remote every time, once per project rather than once per machine, so a package this machine already held was cloned again over the network and a machine with no connection could not fetch at all. dawn now keeps a bare mirror of every source it has seen, under `$DAWN_CACHE` or `~/.dawn/cache`, resolves a reference inside that mirror before it asks the remote, and clones each working tree out of it, so the second project to require a package costs no trip at all and a machine that has fetched a source once can fetch it again with nothing left to reach. And an exported name was global across the whole graph, so two packages exporting one name refused the program and the only fix was to drop one of them. An export now belongs to the package that declares it: a dependency's exported names are renamed `<name>__<alias>` inside that dependency's own files, a file sees them bare only through its own `@import`, and `alias.name()` reaches them from anywhere. Around those two, `dawn tree` prints the graph the manifest and the lock describe without touching the network, `dawn get` asks a checkout which commit it holds rather than taking the stamp beside it at its word, a dependency's own `@dusk` floor is checked where it was only read before, and a `free` of a string literal aborts by name instead of dying by signal.

The packages.

- Exports are import scoped. Every name a dependency exports is renamed to `<name>__<alias>`, with the alias the manifest declared, inside that dependency's own files, and every use within the package follows the rename, so a package goes on calling its own functions by their own names while the merged module holds one distinct symbol per package. Two packages that each export `parse` now coexist, and a project depending on both writes one `@import` per file, or `a.parse(x)` and `b.parse(x)` where it wants both in one place.
- What a file may spell bare is a scope of four layers: its own declarations, every other file of the project, since the project is one package whose files reach each other as they did in 1.14.0, the standard library the project reached, which is every std module any file of the project imported since the project is one scope, and the exports of each dependency the file's own `@import` reached, a leaf import bringing the one symbol it named and nothing else. A dependency's file gets the same shape with the project's layer removed and its own std layer in its place, its own package's exports, the std modules that package's files imported, and what it imports itself, and a project name is not visible inside a package at all. Where two layers answer one name a package's own wins, statically and everywhere, so adding a `@require` cannot change what a name already in the project means, and the rule is symmetric: a package's own export, a sibling file's included, beats any standard library name inside that package's files. What is left is an imported package's export against a std name, which cuts both directions, a std module only a dependency imported and one only the project imported, and there the file that spelled the name picks rather than having one picked for it.
- Bare and qualified reach the same function. `alias.name(x)` resolves through the manifest wherever it is written, with no import at all, and the bare name is what an `@import` of that dependency's module or of one of its leaf symbols adds to the file that wrote it. A local declaration wins over both, so a function, a type, or a binding declared in the file shadows a dependency's export of that name.
- The qualified call folds through the same rename set. `alias.name(x)` becomes `name__alias` when `name` is one of that package's renamed exports and stays the bare name when it is not, so a qualified call still reaches a function whose spelling the rename left alone and `cnum.cabi_tag()` finds a dependency's `export "C"` function under the name the linker knows it by. Resolution follows the order a program reads in, so a `do` step's initializer is resolved before the binder that step introduces and `value <- value()` calls a dependency's `value` and binds the new name after it.
- Six refusals fall out of the scope, each raised at the reference that spelled the name and each naming its fix. `'parse' is exported by both 'a' and 'b'; qualify it` for a name two of the file's imports both export, one of them exporting it through `export "C"`, which the rename leaves alone, being the same refusal as two moved exports. `'maybe_tag' is exported by package 'maybe'; import it` for a name a package this file's own package requires exports and this file did not import. `'leaf_tag' is exported by package 'leaf', which this package does not require; add a @require for it, then import it` for the same shape where the package is only in the build because something else pulled it in, so a manifest line comes before an import. `'trim' is exported by package 'su' and declared by 'lib/std/string.dusk'; import one or qualify it` for an imported package's export meeting a standard library name, the claiming package named and the declaring file spelled beside it, raised in a dependency's own file the same as in the project's. `'app_helper' is declared by the project, not by this package; a package cannot reach it` for a dependency spelling a name only the project declares, which is physically in the program it compiles into and still not a name it was written against. And `'util' names two modules; import the one this file means` for a qualified prefix two of the program's imports gave two meanings, in a file that imported neither.
- Inside a dependency's own file the three shapes are settled apart. Its own package's export, moved by the rename or fixed as an `export "C"`, beats a standard library name of that spelling statically, with nothing raised. Its own package's export against an imported package's export of that name is `'foo' is exported by both 'p' and 'q'; qualify it`, a sibling file's claim and an import's standing on equal footing where neither is the file's own declaration. And an imported package's export against a standard library name is the `import one or qualify it` sentence. Every one of those remedies is a spelling that always resolves, `ca.foo()` for a package's `export "C"` function and `std.vector.vec_len(v)` for the standard library's, so a file with two claims on one bare name qualifies the one it means and builds.
- One package cannot export one name twice. Exporting `foo` as an ordinary export and again through `export "C"` puts two claims on one name inside a single scope, which no import and no qualification can tell apart, so the second declaration is refused, `'foo' is exported twice by package 'p', once as export "C"; a package exports a name once`.
- One refusal is earned by a manifest rather than by a reference. The loader gives a file's own private names a suffix of the form `<file stem>_<number>`, and an alias spelled like one of those would make one symbol out of two declarations, so it is refused at the line that declared it, `package.dawn:4: alias 'json_1' collides with an internal name; choose another alias`, rather than left to surface as a duplicate definition inside someone else's file.
- Five kinds of name keep their spelling, each because a rename would break something the name is standing for. A function declared `export "C"` and every declaration inside a `foreign` block name a C symbol the linker resolves, so the name in the object file is the contract and not dusk's to decorate; inside a package a file reaching its sibling's C export gets that function even where another package's renamed export shares the name, and `ca.foo()` reaches it through the alias. A struct field, a method, and an enum variant are reached through a value whose type already says which package it came from. An exported `bind` and `unit`, spelled `T.bind` or bare, are the shape `do` notation rewrites into and keep it. An unexported pair is outside all of this and keeps the per file renaming it has carried since before 1.14.0, so one file's private `bind` is still not another file's to use, `undefined name 'bind'` exactly as in 1.14.0, and two files each holding a private pair stay legal; only exports are fixed or moved. And a builtin, `print`, `println`, `alloc`, `free`, and `hash` among them, along with every primitive type name, is never renamed, so a package exporting one is still refused by the reservation rule that already governs the name, while an unexported function in an imported file may carry a builtin's name as it always could, since nothing outside that file spells it. Two packages colliding on one of these is the 1.14.0 refusal, `'<name>' is also exported by '<file>'; two packages cannot export one name`, with both files on the line.
- A dependency's own `@dusk` floor is enforced. The compiler reads a checkout's `package.dawn` when its alias is first reached and refuses a floor the running compiler does not meet, under the dependency's own manifest and spelled manifest relative, `dawn_modules/<alias>/package.dawn: package.dawn asks for dusk 9.9, this is 1.14.1`, with nothing resolving through that dependency afterward. 1.14.0 read the line and checked only the project's own, so a checkout written against a newer language failed somewhere in the middle of its source rather than at the line that said which language it was.

The compiler.

- The rename reaches the emitted symbol. Two packages that both export `parse` lower to `@parse__a` and `@parse__b`, and a dependency's monad type carries its scope the same way, `%M__mon`, while the `M.bind` and `M.unit` the `do` rewrite resolves by keep their spelling. A program with no manifest above it has no renamed name anywhere, so every file in this tree and every single file program emits byte identical IR to what 1.14.0 gave it. The IR stays a function of the lock: an alias is manifest text, so two machines with the same `package.dawn` and the same `dawn.lock` emit the same symbols.
- `driver.dusk` is split three ways with no behavior change, the link line and the clang invocation into `driverlink.dusk` and the `--lib` archive and header path into `driverlib.dusk`, 796 lines becoming 222, 224, and 366. Emitted IR is byte identical across the split, which is what the stage ladder proves.

dawn.

- Every source is mirrored once per machine. `dawncache` clones a bare mirror of each source under the cache root, `$DAWN_CACHE` when it names one and `~/.dawn/cache` otherwise, at `<host>/<owner>/<repo>-<hash8>.git`, and every fetch after the first clones its working tree out of that mirror and then points the checkout's `origin` back at the source itself, so running git inside a fetched package talks to the repository the manifest names rather than to this machine's cache. The path is derived from what a source is rather than from how it was spelled: the scheme comes off, a user or a token written before the host is dropped rather than filed away, and the `.git` a clone URL may carry goes too, so `https://github.com/a/b`, the same with `.git`, and `git@github.com:a/b` reduce to one identity and share one mirror. The directories are that identity's components sanitized, a `:port` and every other byte a URL uses for its own structure becoming an underscore and a component of nothing but dots going the same way, so a derived path can neither climb out of the cache root nor mean anything to a shell, and eight hex digits of the identity's hash close the last component, which is what keeps two genuinely different sources the sanitizer would fold onto one name in two mirrors. A `file://` source is a local repository and goes through the same reduction.
- A `get` asks the mirror before the network, and asks it exactly what it would ask the remote. A reference is looked for as a ref of that name and nothing else, `show-ref --verify` on `refs/tags/<ref>` and then on `refs/heads/<ref>`, and what that names is peeled to a commit, the answer `ls-remote`'s peeled line gives. The restriction is the point rather than an optimization of it: a mirror is a local repository and git's revision grammar reads far past a ref inside one, so an unqualified name would also resolve `HEAD`, `main~1`, `v1.0.0^0`, and an abbreviated object id there, none of which `ls-remote` answers, and taking one would pin a lock line to whatever this machine held while the same manifest was refused on a machine with no cache. Every one of those is `cannot resolve` at the mirror and at the remote alike, so a lock records the same commit whatever the state of a cache. A mirror may be stricter than the remote, since a miss falls through to `ls-remote`, and never looser. A miss updates the mirror with `fetch --prune` and asks again before the remote gets a question of its own, and `dawn update` always fetches first, since moving a pin is the one thing a stale mirror must not decide.
- A mirror is locked while it is written. The cache is shared by every project on the machine, so `<mirror>.lock` is made as a directory beside it, which either succeeds or fails and which no filesystem lets two processes make twice, and it is held across a whole cache call rather than around each git command inside one. A process that cannot make it waits and retries, a lock nothing has touched in two minutes belonged to a process that died holding it and is taken rather than waited on forever, and a process that waits out those two minutes without taking or making one gives up and fetches from the remote. Two terminals, or two jobs of a build system, cannot write one mirror between them.
- The cache is an optimization and never a gate. A cache root that cannot be written, a mirror that will not clone or update, and a clone out of one that fails are each reported as `dawn: cannot update the cache for '<alias>' at <url>` with git's own stderr under it, and then stepped around: the fetch goes straight to the remote, which is what dawn did before there was a cache, and the command has not failed. A machine with no `HOME` and no `DAWN_CACHE` has nowhere to keep mirrors and fetches directly every time.
- `dawn tree` prints the graph the project already has. It reads `package.dawn`, `dawn.lock`, and the stamp inside each checkout, and touches nothing else, so it answers the same with no connection. The root prints as its package name and version, each requirement under it as the alias, the source, the reference, and the first twelve characters of the locked commit, indented one step per level, with depth read from each fetched package's own manifest so what a dependency requires prints under it even though the checkout is a sibling in the flat `dawn_modules`. An alias already printed with its requirements under it is not expanded again, so a diamond prints once at depth and a cycle cannot run away. A requirement no lock line pins prints `(not locked)` where the commit would be and one the lock pins with nothing on disk prints `(not fetched)` after it; both are states a build refuses and `tree` reports rather than fails on, so the command exits 0 whatever the project looks like. It takes no arguments, `tree takes no arguments; it prints the graph package.dawn and dawn.lock describe`.
- A checkout is vouched for by what is in it, not by the stamp beside it. A `get` that finds a stamp naming the commit it wants now asks the tree itself, `rev-parse HEAD` for the commit and `status --porcelain --ignored=matching` for whether anything under it changed, the one line naming the stamp as untracked excepted since the stamp belongs to no commit of the package's own. An ignored file counts as a change, since a file the package's own `.gitignore` matches is still a file the build would compile. A tree detached somewhere else, edited by hand, gutted, or no repository at all fails that pair and is replaced, and the line says which happened, `dawn: refreshing <alias>` where a fresh one says `fetching` and an intact one says `cached`. A checkout deleted outright reads as never fetched, as it did before. Both questions are local, so asking them of every dependency on every `get` costs no network.
- The tool has eight commands. `dawn tree` joins `init`, `get`, `add`, `update`, `build`, `run`, and `version`, and the usage prints it between `update` and `build`.

The runtime.

- A `free` of a string literal aborts with a name. A literal, and every other constant the compiler parks in the program image, carries none of the allocation header the generational heap puts in front of a payload, so the retire's read of the size word sixteen bytes ahead of the pointer and its bump of the generation word eight bytes ahead of it landed in read only memory and took the process down by signal with nothing named. Both free entry points now test the pointer against the loaded image, `[__executable_start, _end)`, before they touch the block, and a hit prints `fatal: free of a string literal or other constant; it was never allocated` and aborts, exit 134. Neither the brk heap nor an mmap region ever falls inside that range, so a real allocation never trips the check, and the test is two integer comparisons in a function forced inline, since the runtime is compiled with no optimization flag and a call frame per free is real time in a program that frees millions of strings.
- The bounds are linker defined symbols, declared weak, so the guard is live only where both are defined and a link that defines neither leaves it inert rather than failing, and the whole thing compiles out off ELF, on wasm and on Mach-O, where a free of a constant keeps the older undefined behavior. The spec's memory chapter and its fault list both say so where they used to promise nothing at all.
- `std.fs` stops handing back a literal from a documented owned return. `path_dirname` of a path with no separator returned the constant `"."` and `path_extension` of a name with no dot returned the constant `""`, both documented as fresh heap strings the caller owns, so a caller that freed what it owned freed a constant, which is now a named abort rather than the silent corruption it used to risk. Both fallbacks are heap copies now, and the reference says so: every `path_*` function returns a string the caller frees, on every branch.

The notes.

- A reference the mirror already resolves is not put to the remote, so a tag moved since this machine last fetched it resolves to what the mirror holds. `dawn update` fetches first and is the only command that moves a pin, so the stale answer never reaches a lock line that was not already being rewritten, and a `get` for a reference the machine has never seen goes to the network like any other. The trade is deliberate: it is what makes an offline `get` work at all.
- A diagnostic about a dependency's export may spell the name with its suffix, `parse__maybe`, where the message is built from the merged module's own name rather than from what the file wrote. The suffix is readable and says which package the name came from, which is why it is tolerable, and carrying the written spelling through every message is later work.
- The `import it` and `qualify it` sentences are raised at identifier positions, an expression, a variant path like `Color.Red`, and the head of a struct literal among them. A type annotation carries no span the load pass can raise a diagnostic at, so a dependency's exported type spelled bare in a file that did not import it reaches the type pass and is reported there as an unknown type instead of with the sentence naming the package that exports it. The name is refused either way; which pass says so is what differs.
- A diagnostic raised at an item's own span inside an imported file names the root file rather than the file the item sits in. That predates this release and is not the rename's doing, since every sentence added here is raised at the reference that spelled the name, and it is written down because a program built out of several packages meets it more often than a single file one ever did.
- The rename walk proves its own coverage. A name bearing node inside a renamed file's range that the walker did not reach is a reference the rename could not have moved, which is the one way the pass could fail quietly, so it is checked rather than trusted and raised as `internal: the import rename walk did not reach the name 'x'; report this`. Nothing a program can write produces it, it runs only over a dependency's own files, and `DUSK_RENAME_ASSERT=0` turns it off, which is the escape hatch for getting work done while a walker gap is fixed.
- The alias qualified form is call shaped. `alias.name(x)` reaches a function anywhere, and `alias.Type` in a type position and `alias.Enum.Variant` in a pattern are not spellings the parser takes. A dependency's type, enum, and variant are reached by their bare names through an `@import` of the module or the symbol, which is what every package importing a type already writes.
- The alias namespace is still one namespace across the whole graph, unchanged from 1.14.0. Renaming scopes what a name means inside a file; it does not scope what an alias means inside a project, and the per package alias table remains later work.
- The image guard names a constant in the executable itself. A constant living in the read only data of a shared library the program loaded falls outside `[__executable_start, _end)` and freeing one still dies by signal, since the check is the executable's own range and not a walk of every mapping. Naming that here is the honest version of a guard the release does not extend that far.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 1271 records, 47 new since 1.14.0, with one more rewritten rather than added: the 1.14.0 record that refused two packages exporting one name is now the record that tells them apart by scope. Three are `dawn_*` specials over the same `file://` fixtures the rest of the family uses, with `DAWN_CACHE` pointed inside each special's own work directory so no check reads or writes the machine's cache and every one starts cold: a source fetched once and then moved out of reach, which a second project still fetches because only the mirror can answer it; `dawn tree` over a graph two levels deep, with a deleted checkout marked `(not fetched)` and an unlocked requirement marked `(not locked)`; and a checkout edited, detached elsewhere, and deleted in turn, each replaced by the next `get` with `refreshing` on the line. One static fixture under `tests/dawn/` refuses a dependency whose own `@dusk` asks for a compiler this is not, one `run_raw` record frees a heap string and then a literal and reads the named abort off stderr, and one more frees what every `std.fs` path function hands back and exits clean. The other 42 are the package scope family, built from static fixture projects with no `.git` anywhere so the compiler's whole side of the contract stays an ordinary record. They cover what answers bare, an imported export under its own name, a leaf import leaving the rest of the package's surface alone and the bare name to the standard library, a sibling file reaching its own package's export, and a local binding winning over an import; the refusals, a name reached without importing it, a transitive package naming the `@require` to write first, a package reaching a name only the project declares, a function and an enum two imports both export, and a prefix two files of a program gave two meanings beside the import that settles it; the C boundary, a C export keeping its bare name across a package boundary and staying reachable through its alias, a package's own C export winning over an imported name, a C export and a moving export of one name refused bare in both import orders and both running once qualified, one package exporting one name to C and by suffix refused, and one package exporting one name from two files still a duplicate definition; the standard library cuts, a std name only a dependency imported not picked bare, a project's std import not reaching a dependency's sibling file, a dependency that imported both std and a package exporting one name refused, and a std name and a package's own told apart by qualifying; the `do` protocol, a block over a dependency's monad, a binder spelled like the dependency export it binds, a dependency's block binding from its own import, a root file's pair answering every block, an unexported pair serving no block in a project file, unreachable by a generic `do` from another file, and undefined rather than a collision when two packages each keep one, with the exported pair in two packages refused; and the names that keep their spelling, a dependency exporting a builtin's name refused while its unexported function may take one, an export spelled like the alias its own file imported, and an alias spelled like a private suffix refused at the manifest line that declared it.
