- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.14.0` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (`815e6125e853c11c4fafe9b42e387aa182a414be28a9b824f5c6109e847f7ab7`) and one compiler IR sha256 (`3671f73b40de7932e0bd56493c46c5e6d0b77e9a82df8c16ba34b415074207e4`), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 1271 of 1271 under both of the ladder's own suite runs, each stage building the package tool for its own run. The ratchet holds: the `v1.14.0` release binary builds the 1.14.1 compiler and the package tool, and the freshly built pair passes the full suite. dawn's offline smoke covers the cache, its mirror locking, `tree`, and the refresh alongside the surface it already covered, 105 of 105 checks green. The comment differential holds, 1087 byte equal comparisons over 1184 files and zero failures.

## 1.14.0

The manifest. dusk gets a package system, and the shape of it is one file: a project is a directory holding a `package.dawn`, a package is a git repository with one at its root, and a version is a git tag pinned to a commit in `dawn.lock`. The line between the two tools is the design. dawn owns the network, resolves every reference to a commit, writes the lock, and checks the code out under `dawn_modules/`; the compiler owns the build, reads the manifest, the lock, and the checkouts, and never opens a socket, so a machine that has fetched once builds offline forever and a machine that has not is told which command to run rather than quietly reaching out. An import through a dependency spells the alias the manifest declared and then an ordinary dotted path, `@import maybe.maybe`, so a package reads exactly the way the standard library and a local module already read. Determinism gets its own rule, since fault locations put file paths in the emitted program: a dependency's files register under `dawn_modules/<alias>/<path>` relative to the manifest, never the absolute path of a checkout, so two machines with the same lock emit the same IR. Everything here is additive. A file with no manifest above it compiles exactly as it did at 1.13.3, discovery walks up from the source file rather than the working directory, and the old quoted git import keeps resolving outside a project for one more release.

The manifest.

- `package.dawn` is a directive file, not dusk source, read by its own line sweep: every line is blank, a comment, or a directive with its arguments, arguments split on whitespace, a double quote opening a literal run and the next one closing it with both stripped, no escapes of any kind so a value cannot contain a quote, and a line that is none of the three refused as `expected a directive` with the manifest's path and the line number on it. A `//` opens a comment only at an argument boundary, at the start of a line or straight after whitespace, and inside an argument it is text. That is the rule rather than quote awareness alone, and it is what keeps a URL readable: `@require gh https://github.com/a/b v1` is three arguments and no comment, quoted nowhere. An unclosed quote is `unterminated quoted value; a double quote opened and never closed`.
- Seven directives. `@package <name>` is the package's own name, used by `dusk doc`, by dawn's output, and as the header stem `build --lib` writes from a manifest, letters, digits, `_`, and `-` and nothing else, `@package value '../evil' is not a package name; use letters, digits, _ and -`, since the name reaches a file path as that header stem. It is not what a dependent spells at an import. `@version <text>` is informational, the tag dawn suggests at a release, parsed as nothing. `@dusk <major.minor>` is the oldest compiler the package builds with, compared numerically, an older one refusing by name, `package.dawn asks for dusk 1.99, this is 1.14.0`, and the floor being a major and a minor and nothing else, so a third number is `@dusk value '1.14.0' is not a version like 1.14` rather than a value quietly rounded off. `@root <path>` is the entry file or the library root, relative to the manifest, defaulting to `main.dusk` then `src/main.dusk`, with `no root file; add @root, or put the entry at main.dusk or src/main.dusk` when neither is there and `@root names 'src/gone.dusk', which is not a file` when the path is wrong. `@require <alias> <source> <ref>` declares one dependency per line. `@link <value>` and `@csource <path>` are the package wide form of the two source file directives, folding into the same module wide deduplicated link line in manifest order after the runtime's own C sources, a `@csource` resolved against the manifest that declares it.
- The grammar is strict where a loose reading would cost a build later. Arities are fixed, one argument for `@package`, `@version`, `@dusk`, `@root`, `@link`, and `@csource`, three for `@require`, four for `@lock`, and a miss names the count, `@package takes 1 argument, got 0` and `@require takes 3 arguments (alias source ref), got 2`. A repeated single valued directive is `duplicate @package` rather than an override, and an unknown directive is `unknown directive '@nope'`, so a manifest written against a later release fails loudly instead of building with half of what it asked for. The manifest and the lock hold different directive sets and each says so: `@lock belongs in dawn.lock, not package.dawn`, and `dawn.lock holds @lock lines only`.
- A source is `host/owner/repo`, https assumed, or a full URL beginning `https://`, `git@`, `ssh://`, or `file://`, and a reference is a tag, a branch, or a commit. An alias is an identifier, `'9bad' is not a valid alias; an alias is an identifier like my_pkg`, two names are reserved, `'std' is reserved` and `'dawn_modules' is reserved`, and one alias declared twice in one manifest is `duplicate alias 'x'`.
- `dawn.lock` is generated and committed, one `@lock <alias> <source> <ref> <commit>` line per requirement in declaration order, the commit checked as it is read, `@lock commit must be a 40 character git hash`. `dawn get` writes it, `dawn update` re-resolves it, and nothing else moves a line, so a tag repointed under a project changes that project's build only when someone asks it to. A requirement with no lock line stops a build before any source is read, in `dawn build` and in `dusk build` alike, `'maybe' is not locked; run dawn get`. The lock mirrors the manifest only through dawn, so a `@require` deleted by hand leaves its `@lock` line standing and that line still asks for its checkout, `dependency 'f' is not fetched; run dawn get`, cleared by the same run that would have fetched it.
- The layout is flat. Every dependency in the graph lands at `dawn_modules/<alias>/` beside the manifest, a checkout at the locked commit with the head detached, plus a one line stamp at `dawn_modules/<alias>/.dawn` holding the source, the reference, and the commit. The stamp is the contract a build reads instead of running git: a missing directory is `dependency 'maybe' is not fetched; run dawn get`, a stamp disagreeing with the lock is `dependency 'maybe' does not match dawn.lock; run dawn get`, and a stamped checkout whose contents have been gutted underneath it is `dependency 'util' has no root file; add @root to its package.dawn`, which is the honest answer, since the stamp says what was fetched and the tree says what is there. `dawn init` writes the directory into `.gitignore`, since the lock is what reproduces it.
- Four resolution rules settle the graph, each refusal naming both manifests that asked. dawn reads each dependency's own `package.dawn` and resolves its requirements into the same directory, so a dependency of a dependency is a sibling. A source and reference pair two dependents share is fetched once. One alias naming two sources is `alias 'p' is required from 'github.com/a/p' by package.dawn and from 'github.com/b/p' by dawn_modules/util/package.dawn; one alias names one package`. One alias naming one source at two references takes the root manifest's pin when the root declares that requirement, which is the override, and is otherwise refused with the fix in the message, `alias 'p' is required at 'v1.0.0' by dawn_modules/a/package.dawn and at 'v2.0.0' by dawn_modules/b/package.dawn; add a @require for it in package.dawn to settle the version`. One source and reference reached under two aliases is `'github.com/a/p' at 'v1.0.0' is required under two aliases, 'p' by package.dawn and 'q' by dawn_modules/util/package.dawn; one package has one alias`, since one checkout cannot answer to two names.

The compiler.

- Manifest discovery walks upward from the directory of the root source file to the filesystem root, and the first `package.dawn` on the way up wins. A named file anchors it, not the working directory, so building a file by path from anywhere gives that file the project it actually lives in, and only a command given no file at all starts from the working directory, since there is nothing else to start from. A file with no manifest above it takes the pre 1.14.0 path unchanged.
- An import whose first segment is an alias resolves inside that dependency, under the directory its `@root` names, by exactly the rules a local dotted import already follows, so both the module form and the file plus leaf symbol form work through an alias. An alias followed by one name reads that name as a symbol the dependency's root file exports, `@import greet.greet_line`, the short form a package presenting its surface from one file earns, and it lives beside `alias.file.symbol` rather than replacing it, a name the root does not export refused where it is written, `import 'greet.no_such' names 'no_such', but 'greet' exports no such symbol`. The alias wins over a local file or directory of the same name, which is the point: what an import means is fixed by the manifest, not by the shape of the tree the file sits in, so a new directory beside your source cannot capture an import a `@require` already answers. An unknown first segment is the ordinary unresolved import error.
- Two names are reserved with a manifest above the file or without one. A dotted import through `dawn_modules` is refused, `'dawn_modules.maybe.src.maybe' imports through dawn_modules; import a dependency through the alias its @require names`, since a checkout is reached through the alias that pinned it. And a local directory named `std` no longer shadows the standard library, `'std' is reserved for the standard library; rename the local directory`, where it used to quietly answer an `@import std.io` with whatever the tree happened to hold. Both are raise only and nothing in this tree spelled either.
- A dependency is confined to its checkout. Its `@root` and its `@csource` paths normalize and must land under `dawn_modules/<alias>/`, so an absolute path and a `..` climb are both refused, `dependency 'greet' names a root outside its checkout; fix @root in its package.dawn` and `dependency 'greet' names a C source outside its checkout; fix @csource in its package.dawn`, and its `@link` may name a library and nothing else, `dependency 'greet' names a link path; a dependency may only link a library by name`, since a path there would reach a file on your machine the lock says nothing about. The root package's own `@csource` and `@link` keep the full reading they have carried since 1.4.0, path forms included: your own manifest is yours to write and a dependency's is not. One C source named twice, by a manifest and by a source file directive, deduplicates on the real path and compiles once.
- A dependency's own `package.dawn` and `dawn.lock` are read by the same grammar and their faults are reported at the file they live in, spelled the way every path out of a checkout is spelled, `dawn_modules/greet/package.dawn:3: expected a directive`. A dependency whose manifest does not read does not resolve, so an import through its alias earns that error rather than a vaguer complaint about a module that could not be found. A dependency's own `@require` lines are checked against the lock that drove the resolution too, source and reference both, `dependency 'util' of 'mid' does not match dawn.lock; run dawn get`, which is the case where a checkout moved underneath a lock still describing what it used to ask for.
- A dependency's files carry a manifest relative display path, `dawn_modules/<alias>/<path>`, into every diagnostic and every fault location the program embeds, while the absolute path stays the key the loader deduplicates on. Splitting the two is what makes the IR a function of the lock rather than of where a checkout landed: the same project on two machines with different home directories emits the same bytes. The root package's own root follows in the shape where the compiler chose the file, a bare `dusk build` spelling it `src/main.dusk` whatever directory it ran in, while a command given a file argument keeps the spelling the command line wrote. A display spelling normalizes before it registers, a `./` prefix stripped among the other no ops, so two writings of one path cannot become two strings in the emitted program.
- `dusk build`, `dusk run`, `dusk check`, and `dusk ir` take the manifest's `@root` when given no file argument, so inspecting a project's IR needs no more than the command. With a file argument they behave as they always have, with the manifest found from that file's directory. Neither a file nor a manifest is `dusk: this command needs a file argument, or a package.dawn to build`. None of the four fetches: a missing checkout, a stamp disagreeing with the lock, and an unlocked requirement are each a named error pointing at `dawn get`.
- A quoted git import inside a project holding a manifest is refused, `'example.com/user/repo/mod' is a url import; declare it with @require in package.dawn`, since the manifest is where a dependency and its reference belong. Outside a project the cache resolution still works this release, but nothing fills that cache any more, since dawn fetches into `dawn_modules` now, so the form reaches only a cache placed by hand or left over from an earlier release. It is removed in 1.15.0, and the move is two steps: a `@require` for the repository at the tag you want, and the import respelled through the alias.
- The compiler's manifest diagnostics carry the file that is answerable for them, the manifest's path where the manifest is wrong and the importing file's where an import is, so a fault in a dependency's own manifest points at that manifest rather than at the file that happened to import it. `dusk check --json` reports one the same way it reports any other diagnostic, the manifest in the `file` key and the text in `message`, split rather than run together, so an editor puts a manifest error on the manifest. A manifest has a line but no byte span, so such a diagnostic carries `span: null` beside a new `"line"` key holding the manifest's line number. The key appears only where there is a line and no span, so every diagnostic that had a span before is byte identical to what it was, and the contract's standing rule that a consumer ignores a key it does not know covers the rest.

dawn.

- The tool is rewritten around the manifest and has seven commands. `dawn init [name]` writes `package.dawn`, a root source file, and the `.gitignore` line, with `@dusk` set to the running toolchain's major and minor. `dawn get` resolves the whole graph, clones each requirement, checks it out at the resolved commit, and writes the lock. `dawn add <alias> <source> <ref>` fetches first and writes the requirement only once the fetch succeeds, so the manifest, the lock, and the checkouts move together and a failed add leaves `package.dawn` and `dawn.lock` byte identical to what they were. `dawn update [alias]` re-resolves one requirement or all of them, and it is the only command that moves a lock line on purpose. `dawn build` and `dawn run [args...]` check the lock and the checkouts and hand the manifest's root to the compiler, `run` forwarding its trailing arguments to the program's `argv`. `dawn version` prints the tool version, and `help`, `--help`, and `-h` all print the usage. The old file taking forms, `dawn get <file>` and `dawn build <file>`, are gone: a project is what dawn operates on now.
- git is the whole network layer and the order it is driven in is fixed. A reference resolves through `ls-remote`, a peeled tag first so an annotated tag lands on the commit it points at rather than the tag object, then a plain tag, then a branch, and a 40 hex reference skips the round trip entirely. The fetch is `clone --depth 1 --branch` for a named reference and a full clone for a commit, then a detached checkout at the locked commit, and a clone that fails is removed and retried as a full clone rather than left half fetched. dawn has no client, index, or server of its own, so an `ssh://` or `git@` source uses the keys and agent git already uses, and a `file://` source is a local repository, which is how the suite exercises the entire fetch path offline. A failure quotes git's own stderr rather than replacing it with a summary: `cannot resolve 'v9.9.9' for 'maybe' at https://github.com/choice404/dusk-maybe`, `cannot check out 'v0.3.0' of 'github.com/choice404/dusk-maybe' for 'maybe'`, and `bad source 'not a url' for 'maybe'` for a source that is neither shape.
- Stamps are written per checkout as each one lands and the lock is written last, after the whole graph has, so a fetch that fails partway leaves the lock exactly as it was while everything already stamped stays put and reports `cached` on the next run, which starts from a state the lock still describes. `get` is idempotent: a requirement already locked and already stamped reports `dawn: cached maybe` and touches no network, and a fresh one reports `dawn: fetching maybe from https://github.com/choice404/dusk-maybe`, with `dawn: 3 package(s) ready` and `dawn: wrote dawn.lock` closing the run. Progress goes to stdout, every error goes to stderr under a `dawn: ` prefix with exit 1, and a build prints the artifact the way the compiler does, `[dawn] target/dawn-out/app`.
- The rest of the tool's refusals name the fix. No project is `no package.dawn found from /tmp/x upward; run dawn init`, an `init` over an existing project is `package.dawn already exists in /tmp/x`, an `update` or a removal naming a stranger is `no package named 'nope' is required here`, an `add` of something already required is `'maybe' is already required in package.dawn; run dawn update maybe to move it`, a lock that will not parse is `dawn.lock does not read; fix it or delete it, then run dawn get`, and a command handed arguments it does not take says so, `get takes no arguments; it fetches what package.dawn requires`.
- dawn's version is the toolchain's version from this release forward. One `toolchain_version()` feeds both roots, `dusk version` prints `dusk 1.14.0` and `dawn version` prints `dawn 1.14.0`, and the two can no longer drift apart, where dawn used to carry a number of its own.

The notes.

- The alias namespace is one namespace across the whole graph. A dependency's choice of alias is visible to the project that resolves it, which is what makes the flat layout and the root pin override simple, and the per package alias table that would hide it is later work.
- Only the root project's `@dusk` floor is enforced. A dependency's own floor is read and not checked, so a dependency that needs a newer compiler than the project fails wherever its source needs the feature rather than at its manifest. Naming it here is the honest version of a check the release does not do.
- A version is a git tag and dawn resolves no ranges. A diamond where two dependencies pin one package at different tags is a conflict a person resolves with a pin in the root manifest, and the message says exactly that. What a build depends on is text in two files you can read, and the cost of that is written down here rather than smoothed over.
- There is no integrity check past the commit the lock pins, no vendor mode, and no way to yank a release. The commit pin carries most of the weight, since a repointed tag cannot change a locked build, and the rest is later work.
- `@version` is not compared, ordered, or resolved against. It is the tag dawn suggests when you cut a release and nothing more, so a package that wants meaning in its version numbers gets it by convention rather than by a rule the tool enforces.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 1224 records, 42 new since 1.13.3, split between the tool and the compiler. Eleven are `dawn_*` specials that build their own git fixtures and drive real fetches over `file://` URLs, so the whole path runs with no network: `get` writing the lock and the stamp and doing nothing the second time, a root importing a package through its alias and a run forwarding its own argv, a transitive require landing flat beside the direct one, `update` moving a pin the manifest edited, a missing lock refused by `dawn build` and by `dusk build` alike, a stale stamp refused by alias, `add` writing the require and fetching it, `init` writing the manifest, the `.gitignore` line, and a root that runs, one fetched project copied into two directories emitting byte equal IR, and an alias claimed by two sources refused rather than resolved to whichever arrived last. The other 31 are static fixture projects under `tests/dawn/`, a manifest, a lock, and a prefetched checkout with no `.git` anywhere, which is what lets the compiler's whole side of the contract be an ordinary record: eight that run, the alias import in its module, file symbol, and root file symbol forms, an alias beating a local directory of the same name, a transitive graph, a dependency's own lock cross check, a dependency's C source, and one C source named twice compiling once; and 23 that must fail, a private name reached across a package, an unfetched dependency, a stale stamp, an unlocked require, a url import inside a project, a local `std` directory, a dotted import through `dawn_modules`, a cross package export collision, a `@dusk` newer than the compiler, a malformed manifest line, a missing dependency lock, a duplicate alias, `std` claimed as an alias, a wrong arity, an unknown directive, a dependency with no root file, a leaf symbol its root does not export, a `@root` and a `@csource` escaping their checkout, a dependency linking a path, an illegal `@package` name, a transitive lock mismatch, and a dependency whose own manifest does not read.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.13.3` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (`24c3815716c418758333e65ae24f5ed090b0ba98119e9d8fcadac9c7e9b289b4`) and one compiler IR sha256 (`9a5ff9dbee35c14a8e48339d5e4768f34af36f21c432fd5ac69747bea60ee787`), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 1224 of 1224 under both of the ladder's own suite runs, each stage building the package tool for its own run. The ratchet holds: the `v1.13.3` release binary builds the 1.14.0 compiler and the package tool, and the freshly built pair passes the full suite. dawn's offline smoke covers the rewritten surface, 75 of 75 checks green. The comment differential holds, 1083 byte equal comparisons over 1182 files and zero failures.

## 1.13.3

The expression. dusk adds four spellings and almost no machinery under any of them: `++` and `--` become expressions in a prefix and a postfix form, a `;` ends a statement exactly where a newline does, an `if` used as a value joins the `match` expression 1.10.1 finished, and `c ? a : b` spells that same selection as an operator. The selection pair is deliberately one node, the ternary parsing into the if expression the checker and the code generator already have to judge, so there is one type rule, one ownership verdict, and one escape join across both spellings and no second selection construct to keep in step. Every one of the four is a widening, a shape no legal program could spell before, and the compiler, the standard library, and the tools in this tree spell none of them, so the previous release builds this source unchanged; the adoption sweep comes later. Four smaller surface changes ride along, one of them a change to what an already working program prints: a `bool` renders as `true` or `false`, a `match` written as a statement takes an optional comma after a braced arm, a doc comment ends at its first `*/` rather than nesting, and `export impl` is refused. Alongside all of it the carried ledger empties, and a batch of checker and code generation gaps that used to reach `clang` closes with it.

The surface.

- `++` and `--` are expressions, legal anywhere an expression is: `arr[idx++]`, `arr[++idx]`, `f(i++)`, `return ++n`, an array element, a struct literal field. Postfix yields the value the place held before the step and prefix the value it holds after. The operand is a place whose binding is `mut`, the rule and the messages compound assignment already carries, and the place is evaluated exactly once, so `xs[pick()]++` calls `pick()` once and the read and the store share the index it returned. The operand is an integer of any width, signed or unsigned, wrapping at its own width the way `+ 1` already wraps, and `--` on an unsigned zero wraps to the top rather than going below it. A float, a `char`, a `rune`, a `bool`, and a pointer are each refused with the operator, the type, and the verb in one line, `'++' takes an integer place; float64 cannot be incremented`, `--` saying `decremented` in the same slot and a pointer reading `a managed pointer cannot be incremented`. A non place is `'++' needs a place to write back to: a name, a field, an index, or a dereference` and an immutable place earns the message the assignment forms already give. The statement forms keep the meaning they have carried since 0.4.2, the value discarded with no unused value complaint, and they earn the operator's own messages rather than the arithmetic complaint the compound assignment they fold to would give, so `f++` refuses identically at a statement and inside an expression.
- Evaluation order inside one expression is left to right and written down. With `i` at 1, `i++ + i++` is `1 + 2`, so the expression is `3` and `i` is `3` after it, and `arr[idx++]` indexes at the old value and then bumps. dusk leaves no evaluation order undefined, so a program that steps the same place twice in one expression is answered by the rule rather than by whatever the backend happened to schedule.
- A `;` is a statement terminator equal to a newline. `a = 1; b = 2` is two statements on one line and a trailing `;` before a newline is a terminator the newline was going to supply anyway. It is strict: an empty statement is refused wherever a statement begins, `;;`, a line holding a lone `;`, a `;` written straight after a `{`, and the head of a `do` block's step, `stray ';'; a semicolon ends a statement, and there is no statement before it`, with a `;` after a top level declaration answering to its own message, `stray ';'; a top level declaration does not end in a semicolon`, since the terminator has no meaning there at all. The stray token is consumed, so each one is named once and the read still makes progress. An interface method signature and a `foreign func` declaration keep the optional `;` they have always admitted. There are no insertion rules of any kind, the newline rule stands exactly as it stood, and nothing in the change resembles the automatic insertion JavaScript performs.
- `if c { a } else { b }` is an expression. Each branch's value is its block's tail, the rule a `match` arm already follows, so a branch ending in a bare `match` or a bare `if` value-izes into the selection around it at any depth with no name bound to carry it. An `else` is required in the expression form, `an if used as a value needs an else branch`, since a result slot no branch wrote is the hole 1.10.1 closed for the match expression. Every branch yields a value or diverges, `an if used as a value needs every branch to yield a value or diverge`, no branch may yield a lambda literal, `a branch of an if expression cannot yield a lambda; bind the function to a name outside the if`, and the branches agree on type under the match arm rules, width, signedness, and kind, with a bare literal adapting to the branch beside it, `the branches of an if expression must agree on type`. An `else if` chain carries no new rule, since a chain is an `else` whose whole body is one nested `if`. The statement `if` is untouched.
- The if expression's ownership and escape are the match expression's, not a second model. The result owns only when every branch yields an owner and borrows only when every branch yields a borrow, branches that disagree refused at the odd branch, `the branches of an if expression must agree on ownership of its result`; a borrowed result is refused a `free` and refused a `return`, `cannot return a borrowed if result; move the payload or return an owning call`; inside a generic body the same verdict falls where the `match` form already puts it, since a value of a bare type parameter borrows, so `return if c { a } else { b }` over a `T` is refused with that message by design, `T` being free to instantiate as a managed pointer whose returned borrow would launder into the caller's owner; an inferred bind records the verdict the branches agreed on, so a later `free` of a borrowed selection is refused with no annotation written anywhere; and the value's frame view is the join of the branches, so a view yielded by either branch is a view of the whole expression.
- `c ? a : b` is the loosest operator on the ladder, below `||` and `&&` and above assignment, right associative, so `a ? b : c ? d : e` is `a ? b : (c ? d : e)` and a chain reads as tests taken in order. The parser builds the if expression node with single expression branches, so sema and code generation see one node kind and neither spelling accepts a shape the other refuses. Both branches are required, a `?` with no `:` after its first branch refused at the `?`, `a conditional needs a ':' and a second branch, as in c ? a : b`. The `:` never collides with the annotation colon or a struct literal's field colon, since it is read only after a `?` the parser is already inside.
- A `bool` prints as `true` or `false`. `print`, `println`, `printerr`, a `{}` format hole, and an interpolation hole all write the word where they wrote `1` and `0` before, so `println(x > 0)` prints `true`. This is the one change in the release to what an already working program puts on its output, and it is deliberate: the words are what the value means, and a program reading back its own printed bools reads them now.
- A `match` written as a statement takes an optional comma after an arm whose body is a braced block. The closing `}` ends the arm exactly as the comma would, so `Circle(r) => { ... }` may run straight into the arm below it. An arm whose body is a single expression still takes the comma, since nothing else marks where that expression stops, and the miss names the rule now, `expected ',' after a match arm; an arm that ends in a block may omit it`, where the old refusal reported the token it tripped on without ever mentioning the comma. The rule reads the brace, not the arm's shape, so a tail `match`, `if`, lambda, or `do` ends an arm the same way its own block does, and it holds for a `match` used as a value as much as one written as a statement.
- A doc comment does not nest. `/** ... */` ends at the first `*/`, so prose inside one may spell `cmd/*.dusk` without opening a level nothing closes, which is what the old rule did: a doc comment mentioning such a path swallowed everything after it until some later `*/` happened to balance. The plain `/* */` form nests exactly as 1.6.0 defined it, unchanged, since what it holds is code.
- `export impl` is refused, `an impl takes no export; its methods follow the receiver type`. An impl block declares no name of its own and its methods are reached through the receiver type, so exporting the struct is what makes them callable from another file; the keyword parsed and its flag was dropped on the floor, which read as an export that did nothing. `export foreign` is refused with it, `a foreign block takes no export; its functions are reached by their own names`, since a `foreign` block declares C symbols the linker resolves rather than dusk names a file hands out. The standard library spelled one itself, `std.math`'s block of the C math functions, and the word comes off it here: a foreign name never entered the export set and was never privatized, so nothing about what `std.math` offers or how a caller reaches it moves.

The fixes.

- A `do` block's escape summary stops being cubic in its steps. The summary pass walks the body of each step's lambda three times, once for the frame view, once for the alias table, and once for the free names it reads, and the memo that was supposed to make the repeat walks cheap was keyed by source span. Every lambda a `do` block desugars into nests inside the one the step above it built, so all of them report the same span and the memo answered for the wrong lambda at every level but the outermost, which is a miss on every lookup that mattered: a 50 step block took 11 seconds to check and a 100 step block took 95, the shape of a cost that grows with the cube of the step count. A standalone lambda's summary is memoized on the lambda's own AST node id now, one generation per function transfer so a solver round invalidates cleanly, and the free name scan memoizes with it, which brings the whole thing down to quadratic. Nothing about the answer moves: the escape dump is unchanged for every file in the tree.
- A constructor call of a non generic enum carries that enum's type on the surface pass. It inferred unknown before, and unknown is the answer that lets a question through, so six shapes that are plainly wrong reached `clang` or ran on a value nobody meant: `x: int64 = Opt.Some(1)` is `'x' has a type annotation that does not match its value` now, `return Opt.Some(1)` from a func returning `int64` is `return type does not match the function's return type`, `mut o := Opt.Some(3)` followed by `o = Tag.Hi(1)` is `assignment type mismatch`, `take(Tag.Hi(1))` against a `take(o: Opt)` is `argument 1 has the wrong type`, `println(Opt.Some(7))` is `cannot print the enum 'Opt'; match on it and print its parts`, and `Opt.Some(1) == Opt.Some(1)` is `cannot compare 'Opt'; compare its parts instead`. Every message is one the checker already had; what changed is that a type reaches the site asking. A generic enum's constructor stays a wildcard on the surface, since the named type erases the parameters there, and its instance is typed on the ground pass monomorphization drives, exactly as it was.
- A literal branch is ranged against the type its siblings agreed on, in an `if` expression and in a `match` alike. The agreed type came from the branches that carried one and a bare literal was then stored into the result slot with no range check behind it, so `a: int8 = 5` with `y := if c { a } else { 300 }` built clean and printed 44, the low eight bits of a number the source wrote in full. Both forms range the literal where it is written now, `literal 300 does not fit in 8 bits` and `literal 300 does not fit in uint8` for the unsigned target, the rule any literal at a binding already answered to, and a program that means the wrap writes the cast.
- Four branch shapes that reached `clang` are typed at the slot. A branch that is an `alloc` of a literal size and a branch that is a range index, `c ? arr[0..2] : arr[1..3]`, each left the result slot sized from something other than the value the branch yields. An `alloc` of anything but a literal at a branch tail, `A(d) => alloc(d.n)`, `alloc(n + 1)`, and a branch local `alloc(q.n)` among them, typed its slot as a void pointer, since the size expression was never followed to the type the allocation carries. And a selection whose branches all diverge, both of them a `return`, minted an `int64` slot of its own, which is the wrong slot the moment the selection sits at the tail of another selection's branch or under a binding of a pointer, a string, or a slice, since the inner form then adapted its `i64` into a slot of another type entirely. The slot takes the branch's own type now, the allocation's read off the chain its size expression names, and a selection whose branches all diverge lowers into the slot its position hands it rather than minting one. Every one of the four was a loud death in the backend with no dusk error to its name, never a program that ran and answered wrong.
- A function returning `error` whose body ends in a `match` every arm of which diverges builds. The code generator writes an implicit return after a tail that cannot fall through, and that return was a zero of one fixed shape whatever the declaration said, so a `-> error` function emitted a terminator its own signature disagreed with and `clang` split on it. The implicit return follows the declared return type now, so the terminator matches the signature for every return shape a function can carry.
- A struct field read answers with the field's declared type. Where the base names a non generic struct the checker left the read a hole, and three questions went unanswered behind it: a field the struct does not declare passed check and died later, `(*p).nofield` among the spellings, and is refused now, `'S' has no field 'nofield'`, the message a struct literal naming an unknown field already gave; a field holding a generic container needed a rebinding before its element type was known, where `vec_len(self.items)` inside an `impl` now infers the parameter from the field alone; and an operator that writes a field judged against the hole rather than the type, where `p.f++` on a `float64` field is now refused as the float place it is. Monomorphization learned the same field types, which is what lets `vec_len(self.items)` infer its parameter with the field alone. A field read through a pointer with no dereference comes out with them: `p.n` where `p` is a `*S` had no lowering at all and died in the build as an `unsupported field expression` naming no file and no line, whether or not the field existed, and it is `a field read through a pointer needs an explicit dereference; write (*p).field` now, the read's half of the rule the store form has carried since it was named. The typing narrows what a program can write, and it does so loudly. A read that used to be a hole took whatever width the site around it wanted, and it answers with the width the declaration gives now, so a narrow field earns exactly the messages a narrow local has always earned: `y: int64 = s.n8` is `'y' has a type annotation that does not match its value`, `s.n8 < s.n32` is `comparison mixes int8 and int32; match the widths`, `return self.n8` from a func returning `int64` is `return type does not match the function's return type`, `xs[s.u8]` is `cannot index with uint8; an index is int64, cast the index`, and `s.n8 == 300` is `literal 300 does not fit in 8 bits`. The cast is the way through every one of them, `int64(s.n8)`, and what it replaces is a widening the source never wrote.
- A qualified call folds with the scope around it, which is a miscompile close. The loader rewrites `name.f()` into a free call to `f` when `name` is a module the file imports, since a module's directory name is how an import reads at a call site, and the rewrite asked only whether the name matched an import. A binding of that name lost: `func show(json: Doc) { json.parse() }` inside a file carrying `@import json.core` had the method call on its own parameter rewritten into the module's free `parse()`, checked clean, and printed a value the program never asked for, with nothing in the source to suggest the call had moved. The fold carries the scope now and a bound name wins, so a method call on a parameter, a local, or any other binding is the method call it looks like whatever the file imports, and the module rewrite fires only where the name binds nothing.
- A payload free constructor types with the call form. `Opt.None` inferred unknown where `Opt.Some(1)` now infers its enum, so the annotation, return, assignment, argument, print, and comparison questions above answered one way for a variant carrying a payload and another for the empty one beside it. Both read as the enum they name now, and the generic case is the same wildcard on the surface with the same ground pass instance behind it, so the two spellings of a constructor stop disagreeing about what they are.
- A generic type parameter used as a value is the parameter, not a variant. The reader that resolves a bare identifier consulted the variant table first, so a type parameter whose name matched any variant declared anywhere in the program was read as that variant and refused, `use the qualified form 'E.K' to name an enum value; the unqualified variant name is not one`, at a `sizeof(K)` inside the standard library's own map. The blast radius was a program's own enums reaching into library source it never wrote: `@import std.map` plus an enum of the importer's declaring a variant named `K` or `V` was enough to make `map.dusk` fail to check. The type parameters in scope are consulted first now, so a name a signature declares as a type is that type wherever the body spells it.
- An enum handed where an interface is expected is refused at check. The interface erases to the checker's wildcard in the lowered arena, so the argument, annotation, and return comparisons all agreed with an enum and the value reached code generation, where the dispatched call had no vtable to read and the program died with no dusk error to its name. It is `cannot use the enum 'Opt' as interface 'Sized'; only a struct can implement an interface, so wrap the enum in one` now, which names the fix, since an enum cannot carry the impl either.
- The constant fit family moves out of `tcbin.dusk` into a new `compiler/tcfit.dusk`, every move a verbatim relocation with no behavior change: the literal and constant range checks against a target's width and signedness, the fold that computes a constant expression along with the overflow flag it carries, and the array literal length check all go to the new file, while `tcbin.dusk` keeps the binary operator typing those rules serve. Because a file's private names carry a per file suffix in the emitted IR, moving a private helper shifts that spelling once; the stage ladder re-proves the fixpoint on the new shape and the golden suite pins behavior across it.

The notes.

- A `mut` pointer walked as a cursor over a borrowed tree is not expressible today: rebinding the cursor down the levels reads as a write to a borrowed place, so such a walk is written with a fresh binding per level. The shape is named here and left for a later release.
- A heap slice that crosses an opaque call, a closure held in a struct field for one, is judged conservatively, since the checker cannot see through the callee to know what the call does with the view. A correct program can be refused there.
- An import path is spelled as bare identifiers, so it cannot be quoted and a directory whose name carries a dash cannot be named by one. A package directory goes without dashes until that changes.
- The library search path is the one the compiler resolves for itself, the share directory beside the binary, `DUSK_HOME` where it is set, and a working directory fallback for a source checkout. There is no way to add a second root beside it.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 1182 records, 95 new since 1.13.2: the increment and decrement run over every place shape and every operand position, the index order, the argument and binary order, the place evaluated once, both wraps, the match arm tail, the while condition, and the aggregate literals, with reject twins for a float, a bool, a char, a rune, a pointer, a non place, an immutable place, an immutable chain, and the statement form of each; the if expression and the ternary carried into every carrier a value reaches, a return, a binding, a struct value, a tuple, a string, a float call, an owner result, an interface value, a heap slice, a lambda body, and an `async` await, beside the ternary's polarities, its right association, its expression contexts, and its precedence against assignment, with reject twins for the missing `else`, the missing `:`, a branch that yields no value, branches disagreeing on type and on ownership, and a branch yielding a lambda; the strict semicolon family, a terminator with no statement before it, one after a top level declaration, one at a `do` block step, the statement and loop control forms, and a `return` terminated by one; the arm comma both ways, the doc comment holding a glob and the unterminated one faulting at its own opening, the `impl` block and the `foreign` block taking no export, and the bool print; the diverging tail through both selection forms and through an `error` and a struct return, with the alloc expression, the alloc slot, and the range slot beside them, the borrowed if result refused at a return, at a free, and through an inferred bind, the frame slice escaping through a branch, and the literal branch and literal arm fit at the agreed width; and the typing batch, the constructor family across an annotation, a return, an assignment, a parameter, a print, and a comparison with its payload free twin, an enum where an interface is required, a type parameter name against a variant of that spelling, a generic call pinning its parameter off a struct field, a field the struct does not declare, a field read through a pointer, a struct field read carrying its declared type, and a local binding shadowing an imported module name.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.13.2` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (`bc07ef5036499ff4f29ec411822847bcb2a68fc6c7a112aa75889b971621970d`) and one compiler IR sha256 (`afa3f08e45bc2445931db8309b94e7a24f62eb8263e4b89e95c24df3bf93078b`), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 1182 of 1182 under both of the ladder's own suite runs. The ratchet holds: the `v1.13.2` release binary builds the 1.13.3 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green. The comment differential holds, 1083 byte equal comparisons over 1182 files and zero failures.

## 1.13.2

The terminator. `dusk check` is the command an editor runs on every keystroke, so the one behavior it owes above every other is an answer: a verdict, a diagnostic, or a named refusal, never a walk that does not come back. This release goes after termination directly, a battery of the shapes that could make a pass walk forever or recur past what a stack holds, an explicit depth guard where a walk had none, and a cost measurement of check itself over the shapes an editor actually sends. Alongside it five rules the implementation had left looser than the language means them are closed, four of them raise-only refusals over shapes that could double free or truncate silently, and one the repair of an interface signature that checked clean and then died in `clang`.

The checks.

- The termination battery runs 1956 `check --json` invocations and finds no genuine non termination anywhere in the compiler. The bulk of it is the editor's own diet, 1564 truncated sources cut from the 129 example files, every seventh line and again in the middle of a token, the half written shapes a keystroke actually produces: zero hangs, zero crashes, and 2.86s for the slowest single check. The hostile half feeds what a file should never contain, an unterminated string, block comment, and f-string, an embedded NUL, invalid UTF-8, a 3MiB string literal, a 1MiB single line, an empty file, a file that imports itself, a mutual import pair, and a generic struct whose field names its own instantiation, and every one of them stops with one diagnostic rather than a second error cascading out of the first. The shapes carried as suspected hangs were slowness, not loops: uncaged, each of them finishes in 9 to 11 seconds, and only the outer cage's 10 second timeout ever made them look infinite. Two real crashes did come out of it, a flat binary chain around 3370 terms and a field access chain around 2671 links, each a `SIGSEGV` on a walk with no guard at all, which is what the guards below close; the existing guards held where they already stood, `expression nesting is too deep; simplify the expression` firing at 249 or 498 depending on the shape and `type nesting is too deep; simplify the type` at 500.
- The nesting budget now covers the chain. The parser has long carried one budget of 500 units for the shapes that nest as it descends, a nested block, a nested type, and a nested expression, spent on the way in and released on the way out, reported once per file. A chain escaped it, because a chain nests in the tree without nesting in the parser: a binary operator folds left in a loop, `|>` folds the same way, a postfix field, call, or index link folds over the value beneath it, and a `do` step desugars into one more nested bind. Each of those builds a level the later passes descend recursively while the parser stays flat, so the budget never saw them and the walk ran off the stack, a `SIGSEGV` at roughly 3375 binary terms, at 907 to 924 call, method, and pipe links, and at 2000 `do` steps, with no diagnostic at all. Every link is charged to the same budget now, which puts the ceiling near 496 links at a statement position, and the guard names the shape that spent it: `expression chain is too long; split it across bindings`, `field and call chain is too long; bind an intermediate value`, `do block has too many steps; move some of them into a helper function`, and the existing `expression nesting is too deep; simplify the expression` for a chain of one link that was already at the limit. One budget across all of them is what makes the shapes compose, so a chain buried under nested calls cannot spend its way past the limit by changing shape. The ceiling is far above written code: the longest binary chain in this tree is 10 terms and the longest postfix chain 5 links.
- The guard reports once and the parse stops there. A refused subtree consumes no tokens, so the statement loop used to restart mid construct and every later fault was an artifact of the first: 20000 nested parentheses produced 40001 diagnostics and 2.41GB of output. The guard records its own message before latching a mute, then the statement loops see the mute and stop restarting, so the same file now reports one diagnostic, the one that names the real problem.
- `dusk check --json` over the 6349 line source dusk-lsp is built from drops from 16.6 seconds to 0.43. The cost was one pass: the interprocedural escape summary held 94 percent of a check, because it stored the 64 by 64 relation of which argument's view may be stored into a place another argument reaches as a token string, `";i,j;"` inside a larger string, and every membership question allocated a `StringBuilder` to build the token it then searched for. A call expression asked that question 4096 times without first asking how many parameters the callee has, so a call to a function taking none cost two milliseconds. The relation is 64 rows of an `int64[64]` bit matrix now, one bit per ordered pair, so a query is a word test, the arity bound is the loop condition rather than a filter after the fact, and the representation is sorted and deduplicated by construction, which retires the separate canonicalization the string form needed. Two memos finish it, the flows a summary already computed and the lexical scan that decides which names bind a function.
- Two more quadratics come out with it. A `match` over an enum asked a global string map for the variant table at every arm, so a table of 8000 arms took 10.4 seconds and now takes 0.39, reading the slot directly and memoizing on the type id. Diagnostic rendering scanned from the start of the file to find the line for every diagnostic and copied the whole line to draw the caret under it, which is quadratic in a file that reports many faults on long lines: 8000 nested parentheses fell from 17.8 seconds to 0.014, a file of 8000 invalid nested blocks from 19.1 to 0.14, and an error at the end of a large file from 5.2 seconds to 0.012. A line table is built once and binary searched, and a line wider than 240 bytes renders through a window around the span rather than whole.
- None of this changes what a check says. The `--json` spans and header are byte identical, and the only rendered output that moves is the caret block for a source line past 240 bytes, which now shows a window of that line instead of all of it.

The fixes.

- A map `for` binds both of its names during privatization, which is a miscompile close. Privatization renames a file's non exported top level names with a per file suffix so a bare call cannot reach another file's private helper, and it leaves alone any name the body itself binds. The value binder of `for k, v in m` never entered that bound set, so in a file that also held a private top level of the same name the loop's own `v()` read as a free name and was renamed to that helper: the loop called the file's private function instead of the value the map holds, every iteration, and an importer calling that function got the wrong number back. Nothing reported it, `dusk check` was clean, and the program built and ran. Both binders bind now.
- A `ref` destructuring binding borrows every name it binds. `ref a, b := pair` reads the word with the meaning the single binding gives it, so each binder is a view of what the value already owns, the source stays the single owner that frees, and `free(a)` is refused with `cannot free a borrowed pointer; only its owner frees it` and `move(a)` with `cannot move a borrowed pointer; only its owner can be moved`. The borrow follows the member paths down, so a binder holding a nested tuple hands it to the names a second destructure takes out of that binder: after `ref inner, tag := ((owner, 1), 2)` the `view, count := inner` that follows binds `view` as a borrow. A binder with no ownership to track stays inert exactly as `ref n := 7` does, so a scalar member copies as it always did. Before this release the marker parsed into the statement flags and vanished at the destructure, recording nothing for either name, so a binder that inherited a member's owner record could free a pointer the source still owned and the launder reached the generational check at a later use rather than a diagnostic. The spec named the gap a limit of the word rather than hiding it; the limit is gone, and the scalar inertness is the only one left.
- A tuple literal member that names an owner hands the binder a borrow. `a, b := (owner, 1)` reads `owner` and leaves the ownership where it was, so `free(a)` is refused as a borrow while the single binding refuses the same launder one step earlier, `b: *Box = owner` giving ``cannot copy an owning pointer; bind a `ref` alias or `move` it``, and the rule is the struct embed's spelled for a tuple: packing a live pointer into an aggregate is a read, not a transfer. The transfer keeps its own spelling, `a, b := (move(owner), 1)`, and a member that allocates directly is the binder's own the way any other `alloc` binding is. The record follows the value through a name and through nesting, so `t := (keep, 1)` then `v, m := t` binds `v` as a borrow and a launder through `((p, 1), 2)` survives the inner destructure. Before this release only a member that already named a borrow was recorded, so a member naming an owner handed the binder the owner default and left one allocation with two names each claiming the right to free it.
- A constant an expression of bare literals folds to is ranged against the width that has to hold it. Codegen computes such an expression at `int64` and truncates the result at the store, so the folded value is exactly what the target width holds and ranging it asks precisely whether that store would truncate: `x: uint8 = 128 + 128` is `constant 256 does not fit in uint8` exactly as the written `256` is, `y: int8 = 100 + 100` is `constant 200 does not fit in 8 bits`, `m: uint64 = 0 - 1` is `constant -1 does not fit in uint64; an unsigned type holds no negative value, cast if the wrap is meant`, and the fold follows the literal fit walk into every position it already covered, a call argument written `take(2 * 200)` against a `uint8` parameter reading `constant 400 does not fit in uint8`. The noun is the whole difference from the written form's message, `constant` for a value computed rather than typed, and the caret covers the whole expression, since no single literal in it is the one that did not fit. Every deliberate wrap keeps its own spelling: a suffixed operand stops the fold, so `0u64 - 1u64` computes in its own width and stays legal, a cast stops it, so `uint8(0) - uint8(1)` is the explicit wrap, and a name or a call stops it because neither carries a value the pass knows. A shift, the bitwise operators, and `**` are never folded, since a mask written as a shift is the ordinary way to name a value at the top of a width. The two shapes with no `int64` answer, a zero divisor and the one signed overflow of `int64`'s low value over `-1`, are left unfolded with the runtime, which is where codegen already leaves them. Addition, subtraction, and multiplication that overflow `int64` carry a flag out of the fold rather than a wrapped value, since the wrapped value reads negative and a negative reject is the wrong answer over an unsigned target: `9223372036854775807 + 1` is `9223372036854775808`, which a `uint64` holds, since it holds every `int64` bit pattern and the store keeps the bits the fold computed, and which every narrower type refuses, `constant expression overflows int64 and does not fit in uint8; cast if the wrap is meant`, naming the target and the cast rather than a value because what the fold holds after an overflow is a wrapped pattern and not the number anyone wrote. A fold that never overflowed and simply names a negative is refused at `uint64` exactly as it is at every other unsigned width.
- An array literal that is not the length its type holds is refused. A fixed array is exactly `N` elements wide and a literal builds at its own length, so any other count handed codegen a differently sized aggregate: `mut r: int64[64] = [0]` emitted a `[1 x i64]` store into a `[64 x i64]` slot and died in `clang` with no dusk error to its name, the shape a person writes meaning a zero fill. The count is checked now and the message names which way it went, `the array literal has 1 element(s) but int64[8] holds 8; a short literal is not padded, write every element` for a short one, since dusk has neither a fill form nor an initializer less declaration to point at, and `the array literal has 3 element(s) but int64[2] holds 2; drop the extra elements or widen the type` for a long one. The check lives in the one target directed literal walk, so it lands at every position that walk reaches, an annotated binding, a destructured binder, an assignment, a return, a call argument, a struct literal field, and a `collector<T>` mint, and at any nesting depth inside another literal. The three shapes the rule must leave alone keep working: a literal coerced to a slice takes its own length, a `char[N]` filled by a string literal counts bytes under its own rule, and a fixed array bound from another fixed array value is not a literal at all. A wrong count reports once and the elements written are still checked against the element type.
- A field access or an index whose base has neither is refused at check. A scalar carries no member and no element, so `a.f` and `a[0]` over an `int64` had no lowering at all: both passed `dusk check` and died in code generation as an `unsupported field expression` or an `unsupported index expression` that named no file and no line, the worst shape a diagnostic can take. Each names its legal set now, `cannot access a field of int64; a field access takes a struct, an array or a slice '.len', or an error '.message'` and `cannot index int64; an index or a range slice takes an array, a slice, a string, or a pointer`, and a string gets its own answer for the field read a program actually reaches for, `cannot access a field of string; a string has no fields, its byte count is str_len(s)`. The legal shapes are pinned beside the rejects so neither widens onto one: a struct field and a nested chain of them, a field through a dereference, `self`, a qualified enum variant, a method call, an array's and a slice's `.len`, an error's `.message`, an index into an array, a slice, a string, and a raw pointer, a range slice of an array and of a string, and a field or an index inside a generic body where the type is still a hole the ground pass judges after monomorphization. Both refusals cover an assignment target as well as a read, since the left side infers through the same rule.
- A match over a scrutinee the surface pass cannot name is judged for coverage. A constructor call, a binding a constructor initialized, and a struct field read each infer unknown on the surface, and the coverage check returned on an unknown scrutinee, so `o := Opt.None` followed by a `match o` whose only arm names `Some` built clean, took no arm at run time, and kept going with nothing bound. The enum is recovered from the scrutinee expression now, through the enum a binding was initialized from, through a qualified constructor call, and through a field, index, or dereference chain that resolves to one, and coverage is judged whenever it resolves: the same match is `non exhaustive match, missing variant 'None'`. A bare qualified constructor such as `Opt.None` is a field expression rather than a call, so its binding recorded no enum at all; both constructor forms are recorded now. A scrutinee that still does not resolve returns as before, and the accept twin proves a covered match and a wildcard arm stay legal.
- The same resolved enum judges a match's arms, not only its coverage. Three more questions hung on a scrutinee type the surface pass could not name, and each of them answered by letting the arm through. A pattern binding the wrong number of payload names, `Some(a, b)` over a variant carrying one field, was accepted with the second name bound to nothing, and is `variant pattern 'Some' binds 2 of 1 payload field(s)` now. An arm naming a variant the scrutinee's enum does not declare was accepted and died in the build instead, and is `'Opt' has no variant 'Circle'` now. And a payload carrying variant named alone, `match Opt.Some { ... }`, is a constructor rather than a value: it typed as the surface unknown, reached codegen, and lowered to a zeroed enum that still matched its own arm, so the match bound and printed a payload nobody supplied. It is `'Opt.Some' takes 1 argument(s), so naming it alone is not a value; call it with its payload` now. The binder's type comes from the resolved enum's own variant slot with them, so the four judgments a match makes all read one enum.
- An enum's own name is reserved at every binding position. Code generation reads `A.Some` as a constructor off the base identifier's spelling alone, with no scope to ask, so a binding that took an enum's name made a field read through it lower to a zeroed constructor of that enum: the checker's field arm asked the scope and its constructor and scrutinee readers agreed with codegen, the two answers disagreed, and the program printed a value nobody wrote. Through a parameter the same shape passed check with a payload of the wrong type, since the body's `Opt.Some` read as the declared enum's constructor whatever the caller passed. A `let` or annotated binding, a destructure binder, a parameter, a lambda parameter, both binders of a `for`, a `match` arm's payload binder and its catch all binder, and an `await` binder each refuse the name now, `'Opt' is an enum name; a binding cannot take it, because a field read through it names that enum's constructor, so rename this binding`. The reservation is the enum's name and nothing more: a variant name still binds freely, since no lowering keys on a variant alone, a struct field spelled like a variant reads as the field it is, and a payload free arm naming a variant of the scrutinee's own enum names that variant rather than binding, even where another enum carries the same spelling. Which of the two readings a payload free arm takes is the scrutinee's enum to answer, so an arm over a scrutinee that cannot be named on the surface, a type parameter inside a generic body, a generic call's result, or a binding of either, waits for the ground pass to decide once the enum is concrete, and only a real catch all binder spelled with an enum's name is refused.
- A variant payload is read out of the enum that declares it. Four reads answered from a table keyed by the bare variant name, where the last enum declared under a name won: the payload of a constructor call, the binder a `match` arm introduces, the alias link that ties a payload binder to its scrutinee, and the constructor arm of the summary pass. Two enums that both spell `Some` were enough to cross them, so one enum's payload was judged by the other's field types, and an `int64[]` payload read as an `int64` carries no view at all: a slice into a dead frame passed the escape walk in a constructor, `a slice into a local array escapes its frame; put the backing on the heap`, out of an arm binder the same way, through the payload alias link, `this returns a pointer to an object that stores a view of the current frame`, and across a call boundary, `this call may return a view of argument 1, which views the current frame`. Each read asks its own enum now, the type pass through the enum a qualified constructor `A.Some` names, or the enum the scrutinee resolves to, which now reaches an inferred constructor binding and a field or dereference chain as well as a scrutinee whose type is written out, the summary pass through a table keyed by the enum and the variant together. The qualified spelling is the only one that reaches these reads, since a bare constructor call is already refused by the call check, and a callee whose enum does not resolve keeps the name keyed answer it had. Where the scrutinee's enum does not resolve and the variant name belongs to more than one enum, no payload signature is trustworthy, so the escape side reads none: every arm binder is treated as carrying the scrutinee's view and as aliasing it, and every constructor argument is treated as carrying its own, which costs a scalar binder nothing and keeps the frame view a slice binder was silently dropping. An outer match's payload binder, a tuple destructured name, and a generic struct field whose declared type is the type parameter are each unnameable in that way and each judged conservatively now. A variant name only one enum declares still answers by name exactly as before, so the pessimism fires only where the name is genuinely ambiguous. The record a binding carries widened to match: a copy of a name inherits what that name recorded, a read out of a place, `x := b.o`, `(*bp).o`, and `xs[i]`, records the enum its declared type names, and the payload free form `Opt.None`, a field expression rather than a call, records its enum too, while a `mut` binding records nothing at all, since a later assignment may replace its value with another enum's. The lookup stops at the scope that declares the name rather than walking past it, so a binding that shadows an outer one holding a different enum no longer answers with the outer enum, which had both refused a legal match and typed a payload from the wrong enum. A binding that names itself, `o := o` inside a nested block, asks the scope outside its own, since the binder is declared before its value is read and stopping at the name being defined answered with nothing and left the match unjudged. Both declaration orders are pinned, since the name keyed read was right by accident in one of them, and the accept twin proves two enums sharing a variant name stay legal to construct and match.
- A name that shadows a variant is not its constructor. The escape model asked only whether a bare callee's name appears in the variant payload table, so a top level function, a local closure, or a parameter that happens to be called `Some` was modeled as that variant's constructor, and the variant's payload types then decided which arguments carry a view: a scalar payload dropped a slice argument's frame view and the call handed back a dead frame unreported, `a slice into a local array escapes its frame; put the backing on the heap`. The summary walk resolved the same bare name against the variant table before its own locals, so a callee carrying the shadowed name was credited with returning nothing derived from its argument, `this call may return a view of argument 1, which views the current frame`. Both sides read a local binding, a parameter, and a top level function first now, the same two guards the call check already applies before it names an unqualified constructor, and the accept twin proves a function and a closure repeating variant names still call and run.
- The capture scan sees scope. It gathered the names a lambda body uses and the names it binds into one flat set, so an inner lambda's parameter named like an outer local, or a `let` of that name inside a nested block, made the outer name read as bound and the capture vanish. A closure over a frame slice then stored through a parameter, `(*dst).f = g`, looked frame free and built, leaving a field holding a closure over a dead frame. Each construct binds into its own scope now, so the store is refused where it always should have been, `a view of the current frame is stored into a place reachable through parameter 1, which outlives it; put the backing on the heap`, in both the inner parameter and the nested block spelling. The accept twin holds the line the other way: a name whose only occurrence is an inner lambda's own parameter captures nothing, and the store through the parameter stays legal.
- A map `for` binds both of its names in the capture walk. The walk bound the key and left the value binder unbound, so a closure inside a `for k, v in m` body that reads its own `v` looked like it captured an enclosing binding of that name and was charged with a capture it never makes, refusing a legal program. All four walkers that track which names a body binds bind both now, the capture scan, the summary walk, the escape capture walk, and the scan that decides which names bind a function, so the value binder shadows an enclosing name of its own for exactly the body it belongs to.
- The collected mint descends an enum constructor. The walk that asks which bindings are embedded in a collected value followed an array, a tuple, and a struct literal but stopped at a constructor call, so `cells: Held[1] = [Held.Some(p)]` linked nothing and the frame view held behind `p` never reached the mint's guard, leaving the deep copy to immortalize the array while the pointee it names dies with the frame. A constructor's payload arguments are embedded like a literal's members now, so the mint is refused, `a collected slice element holds a pointer to an object that stores a view of the current frame; the collected block outlives the frame, so heap own the pointee or collect it first`. Only a constructor descends here; every other call still answers through its summary at the sites that model calls, and the accept twin proves a wrapped pointer whose pointee holds a heap backed slice still collects.
- An interface method signature may name a generic instance. `boxed() -> Box<int64>` and `take(b: Box<int64>) -> int64` now build and dispatch: monomorphization grounds each interface declaration in place, so the vtable thunk, the slot that receives a dispatched call, and every impl body agree on one mangled type. Before this release the declaration kept its type as written while the impls were ground, and codegen sizes the thunk and the result slot from the declaration, so the module reached `clang` with an `i64` argument against a `Box$int64` operand and split there after checking clean. Both dispatch shapes are pinned, the field read straight off the call and the binding that stores the returned struct first, with two impls proving the vtable still picks the right thunk. The grounded node replaces the original at its own arena id rather than being appended, since codegen finds an interface by a name scan that stops at the first hit.
- An interface declared with type parameters of its own is refused. `interface Speaker<T>` is rejected, `the generic interface 'Speaker' is not supported yet; spell its methods over concrete types, since an interface value erases the type it holds and cannot be monomorphized`: an interface value is a data pointer paired with a vtable pointer and erases the concrete type behind them, so no dispatch site can pin `T` and every impl shares one vtable slot whose signature has to be a single concrete type. The declaration was previously parsed and treated as though its parameter were `int64`, building a thunk that disagreed with any impl spelling another type. The neighboring rule is written down at the same time: a bare interface is still refused as a generic type argument, while an interface behind a constructor is a carrier of interface values and stays legal, so `Box<Speaker[]>`, `Box<Speaker[2]>`, and `Box<*Speaker>` are accepted at any nesting depth, and the ordinary conversion rules still apply inside the carrier, a slice of concrete structs handed to a `Box<Speaker[]>` refused with `cannot pass a slice of 'Dog' as a slice of interface 'Speaker'; a slice of concrete values cannot be reinterpreted as a slice of interfaces`.

The editor.

- dusk-lsp, which ships from its own repository, bounds the `dusk check` it spawns: every spawn runs under a timeout and the spawns themselves sit behind a cost paced gate, so a keystroke can never leave an unbounded check running behind the editor. The behavior and its knobs are documented there.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 1087 records, 75 new since 1.13.1: the chain and nesting guards with their cascade pin, the literal fit family through aggregates, destructuring, match arm tails, operand positions, and folded constants, the array literal length at every position, the scalar field and index rejects with their legal set pinned, the interface signature over a generic instance in both positions and the carrier forms, the ref destructure and tuple member ownership pair, the shared variant name family in both declaration orders across the constructor, the match binder, the collector mint, and the summary pass, the inferred constructor coverage, the bare callee named like a variant, the scoped capture walk, the map pair loop binder through every walker and the privatization pass, the enum name reservation at every binding position with the generic arm exemption, and the constant overflow pair.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.13.1` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (`85aea42cd294cc9f5f6c8a03672752ecabd9a3ed3b935b8dc557815e15d868b2`) and one compiler IR sha256 (`17740b4021df5e52c63e107b0d44eda1ba38cc4bc1c7debbaf7d027db38ec615`), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 1087 of 1087 under both of the ladder's own suite runs. The ratchet holds: the `v1.13.1` release binary builds the 1.13.2 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green. The comment differential holds, 1005 byte equal comparisons over 1086 files and zero failures. The escape summary's new representation was proved against the old one by running both compilers over the same tree and comparing every `dusk esc` dump byte for byte, the only differences being the programs this release deliberately rejects or renders differently.

## 1.13.1

The split and the audit. No language surface changes and no behavior changes: this release reshapes the compiler source for the work ahead and squares the documentation with the implementation.

The split.

- The eight compiler files that had grown past 800 lines are each split along a domain seam, every move a verbatim relocation: `parseexpr.dusk` keeps the expression grammar and `parsestmt.dusk` takes the statement, block, control, and `do` grammar; `gencall.dusk` keeps call dispatch and `genfcall.dusk` takes the foreign and ABI call lowering; `astdump2.dusk` keeps the statement renderers and `astdumpexpr.dusk` takes expression rendering; `loadfold.dusk` keeps the shift and qualified call walkers and `loadcopy.dusk` takes the AST copy walker; `tccall.dusk` keeps call inference dispatch while `tccallshape.dusk` takes the builtin, cast, and constructor shapes and `tccallcheck.dusk` the task, variadic, callback, and alias checks; `tciface.dusk` keeps interface conformance and `tcifaceforeign.dusk` takes the foreign signature and async boundary checks; `tcesc.dusk` keeps the escape roots and walks and `tcesccap.dusk` takes captures, alias binding, and collect checks; `tcexpr.dusk` keeps expression dispatch and match walking and `tcexprprep.dusk` takes aggregate and lambda inference. The largest compiler file drops from 1354 lines to 543. Two shared render helpers took an `ad_` prefix to clear an existing export name; nothing else about any function changed.
- Because a file's private names carry a per file suffix in the emitted IR, splitting shifts that spelling once; the stage ladder re-proves the fixpoint on the new shape and the golden suite pins behavior across it.

The audit.

- The spec and the standard library reference were read against the implementation and corrected where they disagreed. The async network functions are documented as the `async func` declarations they are and their example awaits its calls; `proc_read_line` and `proc_close` take the `*Proc` they really take; `parse_float` is documented as the builtin it is rather than a `std.string` export; the doc comment section agrees with itself about an `impl` head.
- `ref` bindings get a spec section of their own after shipping since 1.7.0 with only a diagnostic mentioning them, including their two named limits as they stand today.
- The reference gains the tables it was missing: `std.async.future`, `std.async.loop`, `std.async.time`, the five `std.concurrent` modules, `vec_set`, and the positional map accessors container loops lower onto. The module table names every module that ships. Math signatures carry their parameter types, and the status heading no longer names a version.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 1012 records, unchanged from 1.13.0: a source reshape with a green suite before and after is the whole claim.
- The stage ladder re-fixed on the split shape: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (`075b8601c63f7a405ef15221c9d37c8463a3e11826734c1e82d3e2627b164568`) and one compiler IR sha256 (`c17836d95c87c68fd280a846a69347b2ca43ec1ff12a365ef0b65c4c2f1d28f8`). The ratchet holds: the `v1.13.0` release binary builds the 1.13.1 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite.

## 1.13.0

The unsigned width. The four unsigned integer types dusk reserved through the whole bootstrap become real surface: `uint8`, `uint16`, `uint32`, and `uint64`, the `u8` through `u64` literal suffixes, and four new cast builtins.
