- The impl-self receiver rules and the mut tuple reassignment, both fixed by earlier lines without ceremony, are pinned: mutation through a value receiver persists, a pointer receiver mutates its pointee, returning `self` where a pointer is expected still rejects, an `impl` on an enum still rejects, and the tuple that mixes an array literal member with a later slice reassignment builds and runs.
- A hand-rolled deep free of a node tree is inexpressible, and the spec now says so instead of leaving the refusal to be discovered: a parameter borrows, a container read borrows, a match payload pointer borrows, and only the binding that received an `alloc` may free it, so the walk that visits every node never holds the right to free one. The new ownership section names the boundary and the answer the language already ships, the collected heap, where a `collector<T>` reclaims unreachable cells with no `free` at all. A payload string stays freeable. The runtime keeps handing a never-allocated pointer to libc `free` undetected; adding a registry to name that fault would tax every allocation for a diagnostics-only win, measured and declined.
- `@param` may now name a generic type parameter, so `func id<T>(x: T)` documents `T` beside `x` instead of drifting, and a doc comment before an `impl` head binds to the impl, renders as its header prose, and appears in the JSON model; the impl object's `doc` key is the one additive schema change. A doc before a `foreign` block still dangles, deferred until the boundary grows a doc story of its own.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 741 records, 13 new since 1.6.1: the escape hole's reject and its clean twin, the projection and pointer-array carriers, the two declaration-position rejects, the borrowed-free boundary, the ledger pins, and the doc fixes with their JSON twins.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.6.1` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (01aec1ee...) and one compiler IR sha256 (b42e5d86...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 741/741 under the ladder's own suite runs.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.6.1` release binary builds the 1.7.0 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite, which is also the proof the compiler, standard library, and tools all still build under the tightened escape check. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green. The comment differential still holds, 682 rewritten variants byte equal.

## 1.6.1

The doc comment. A block comment that opens with `/**` now binds to the declaration it precedes, and the new `dusk doc` command renders a module's documentation as markdown or, with `--json`, as a stable JSON model for tooling. The doc carries prose only, the summary, the parameter and return descriptions, and examples, while every fact, the names, the types, the whole signature, comes from the declaration itself, so the documentation cannot drift from the code, and where the prose does contradict the signature the command refuses to emit and says why.

The doc comment.

- `/**` whose next byte is neither `*` nor `/` opens a doc block: `/**/` stays an empty plain comment and `/***/` or a `/****` banner stays plain. Everything else about it is an ordinary block comment, nesting, terminating, and evaporating by the 1.6.0 rules, so a doc block changes nothing about how a program compiles: `check`, `build`, and `ir` output is byte identical with the docs present or stripped.
- Binding follows the token gap. A doc block binds to the item whose first token comes next, `export` and other modifiers included; blank lines and plain comments in between do not break it, and a same line doc binds too. A doc before the directive prologue documents the module; in a file with no directives a leading doc binds to the first item. Bindable targets are functions, structs, enums, interfaces, interface method signatures, impl methods, and monad methods. Anything else dangles, and a dangling doc is an error the doc command reports with a caret on the block.
- Three tags, recognized at line start after the gutter strip: `@param name description`, `@return description`, and `@example`, which opens a verbatim block where an unknown `@` word is content and a line starting with one of the three tag words ends the example. The first paragraph is the summary, later paragraphs the body. Both the aligned `*` gutter style and the plain indented style normalize cleanly.
- `dusk doc <file>` emits deterministic markdown, every item in source order with its signature rebuilt from the declaration, undocumented items listed with the signature alone, prose verbatim. An emitted code fence grows one backtick past the longest backtick run inside its content, so an example carrying a fence of its own renders intact. `dusk doc --json <file>` emits the same model as JSON with a fixed key order, two space indent, and null for absent docs, the contract a language server consumes. Both read the single file with no import loading and no type check.
- The refusals: a dangling doc, an unknown tag outside an example, an `@param` naming no parameter or repeating one, an `@return` on a `void` function, and an `@param` or `@return` on a module, struct, enum, or interface doc. Each is located at its real byte inside the doc block, everything is reported at once, the exit is nonzero, and stdout stays empty, so a doc pipeline never consumes half a page. Partial documentation is deliberately not drift: a function may document some parameters and omit `@return`, and only a claim that contradicts the signature refuses.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 728 records, 18 new since 1.6.0: attachment and dump stability, the markdown and JSON surfaces, every refusal, the directive-less binding, and the fence growth.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.6.0` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (7795ce68...) and one compiler IR sha256 (12315ba9...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 728/728 under the ladder's own suite runs.
- Doc blocks are proven compile invisible: a doc-bearing module and its plain-comment twin emit byte identical IR, and the 1.6.0 comment differential still holds, 682 rewritten variants byte equal, zero divergences.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.6.0` release binary builds the 1.6.1 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green.

## 1.6.0

The block comment and the second target. dusk carried exactly one comment form since 0.1.0, the `//` line comment, and the spec never wrote even that one down. This release adds `/* */`, nesting the way commented out code needs, and gives the spec a Comments section that records both forms. Alongside it, `dusk ir` learns to cross-emit: `ir --target=wasm32 <file>` produces the module's LLVM IR for wasm32-wasip1, the shape a wasi toolchain links and the form a browser playground builds from, while every other command keeps the native triple untouched.

Block comments.

- `/*` opens a block comment and `*/` closes it, and they nest: each `/*` inside an open comment opens another level, so a block comment can comment out code that itself contains block comments. The comment evaporates at the lexer and produces no token; an unterminated comment is a lexical error reported at its opening `/*`. Inside a block comment only `/*` and `*/` are significant, so a quote or a `//` inside one is inert, and symmetrically a `/*` inside a string, char, or rune literal is text.
- A block comment that contains a newline terminates a statement exactly the way the newline would, and one that opens and closes on a single line does not, so wrapping a comment around code never glues two statements together. Both directions are pinned by goldens, since this is the one edge where a comment could silently change what a program means.
- The directive prescan, the line sweep that collects `@paradigm`, `@import`, `@link`, and `@csource` before lexing, carries the comment state too: it threads the nesting depth across lines with a line local string state, so a directive inside a block comment is dead in every consumer, the scan dump, the loader, and the link line alike, and a quoted `/*` in a directive argument cannot swallow the lines below it. An elided comment leaves one space behind, so a comment separates words exactly the way it separates tokens: `@link/* note */"m"` still links libm, and a comment splitting a directive word fails loudly as an unknown directive rather than reassembling.
- `tools/comment-differential.sh` proves the feature invisible: it rewrites every line comment in the example corpus into block form without moving any token's line or column, in a per line wrap and a run merge variant, and asserts the emitted IR is byte identical.
- Thirteen new goldens cover nesting, adjacency, the statement break rule in both directions, comments among directives and match arms and types, both unterminated shapes, the dead directive on the scan and loader surfaces with its live twin, the shielded quoted `/*`, the tight elision, and the split directive reject.

A second target.

- `dusk ir --target=wasm32 <file>` emits the module for `wasm32-unknown-wasip1`, the canonical spelling clang normalizes to. The entry point is the difference: wasi-libc's crt1 calls `__main_argc_argv` where a native C runtime calls `main`, so the argc/argv main wrapper takes the entry symbol for the target, and a nullary `main` gets a wrapper that accepts and drops the two wasi arguments. The native path emits through the same single named default triple it always has.
- `runtime/wasm_shim.c` carries the wasm side of the runtime: inert stubs for the process and shell layer wasi has no shape for, and a stand in for `collect.c`, which cannot compile for wasm while setjmp waits on the exception handling proposal, so the anchor prologue is a no-op and every pointer reports non collected, leaving the generational heap to own all memory. `runtime/runtime.c` declares `popen`/`pclose` under `__wasm__` so it compiles against wasi-libc's stdio, which omits them. The shim's whole body is fenced to the wasm target, so a native link that sweeps the runtime directory with a glob, the bootstrap and the AUR seed link both do, compiles it to an empty object instead of colliding with `collect.c`. Emission is the supported surface; the wasi link that consumes the shim happens outside the compiler, with a wasi sysroot, which keeps the toolchain list unchanged.
- `std.os`'s errno read is renamed `os_errno`. The wrapper's old bare name collided with the very C symbol it reads on a target whose libc declares `errno` as a symbol of its own; the new name keeps the wrapper linkable everywhere. `errstr` is unchanged, `std.fs` and `std.process` follow the rename at every call site, and the spec and stdlib docs record it.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 710 records, 13 new since 1.5.3, all for the block comment surface.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.5.3` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (8238eb6e...) and one compiler IR sha256 (627ffcb4...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 710/710 under the ladder's own suite runs.
- The comment differential holds: 682 rewritten variants across the 712 example files emit byte identical IR, zero divergences.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.5.3` release binary builds the 1.6.0 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green.
- The wasm emission is smoke verified at the IR level: the cross emitted module carries the wasip1 triple and the `__main_argc_argv` entry, and the native module is unchanged. No golden executes a wasm module, since running one takes a wasi runtime the toolchain list does not include.

The string toolkit. `std.string` grew up serving the compiler, so it had parsing, a builder, and the foreign bridge, but not the everyday manipulation set a program reaches for first. This release adds it: searching from the tail, ordering, trimming, splitting and joining, replacing, repeating, and ASCII case folding, twelve functions, all pure dusk over the existing builder, no compiler or runtime change.

The string toolkit.

- `ends_with` mirrors `starts_with` at the tail; `str_rfind` finds the last occurrence the way `str_find` finds the first, the empty needle matching at the end; `str_cmp` compares by unsigned byte order and returns the negative, zero, positive shape `vec_sort`'s comparator takes, so a vector of strings sorts with no glue.
- `trim_start`, `trim_end`, and `trim` strip ASCII whitespace from the named ends, always returning a fresh heap string; `repeat` concatenates a string with itself n times, zero or negative n yielding the empty string; `replace_all` replaces every non overlapping occurrence left to right, an empty pattern copying the input rather than looping.
- `str_split` splits on every occurrence of a separator, adjacent separators yielding empty strings and a leading or trailing separator a leading or trailing empty string; an empty separator yields one copy of the input. `str_join` is its inverse over a `*Vector<string>`. The pair is why `std.string` now imports `std.vector`; both the vector and its elements are fresh allocations the caller owns. The names carry the `str_` prefix because a bare `join` is the thread builtin's call form, the same reserved set `hash` joined one release ago. One consequence of the import: since nearly every module imports `std.string`, a program that imports any of them now sees the `vec_*` names too, so a program defining its own `vec_len` or `Vector` fails loudly with a duplicate definition where it compiled before; rename the private copy.
- `to_upper` and `to_lower` fold ASCII letters only: a byte at 128 or above passes through untouched, so a multibyte UTF-8 scalar survives intact, and a full Unicode case fold stays out of the module, the same posture the unicode tables took.
- `examples/string_toolkit.dusk` exercises every function including the edges, an all whitespace trim, a trailing separator split, a repeated needle searched from both ends, an empty pattern replace, and a multibyte string through the case fold; `examples/string_toolkit_reject.dusk` pins a wrong typed argument.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 697 records, 2 new since 1.5.2 for the toolkit and its reject.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.5.2` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (e2a7d4be...) and one compiler IR sha256 (9af74291...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 697/697 under stage1 and stage2 alike.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.5.2` release binary builds the 1.5.3 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green.

## 1.5.2

The generic map. `std.map` was keyed by strings alone since it first shipped; it is now generic over both its key and its value, `Map<K, V>`, with K drawn from the hashable set the `hash` builtin defined one release earlier: an integer of any width, a `char`, a `rune`, or a `string`. A struct, pointer, or float key is rejected by name. Every existing map is spelled `Map<string, V>` now, and the compiler itself, the heaviest map user in the tree at more than seven hundred annotation sites, migrated with it. The split from 1.5.1 is deliberate sequencing: the map uses `hash`, so `hash` had to be one release old before the map could, keeping the previous release's binary able to build the current source.

The generic map.

- `lib/std/map.dusk` threads K through the whole structure: `keys` is a `*raw K`, the insertion order record a `*Vector<K>`, and every function takes and returns K where it took a string. The key hash is the `hash` builtin and key equality is `==`, so a string key hashes and compares by content exactly as before, byte for byte through the same polynomial hash, and a scalar key by value. The open addressing, the linear probe, the half full grow, the remove backshift, and the insertion order iteration are unchanged. `map_hash` stays exported as a thin wrapper over `hash` for one release.
- The migration to the two parameter spelling is uniform: every `Map<` in the compiler, the standard library, and the examples became `Map<string, ` in one pass, and no call site changed at all, since `map_new()` and every accessor infer K and V from the annotated binding or the map argument. `map_keys` callers keep working unchanged, since every existing key type is still string.
- Six call sites in codegen needed one local rebind each: a map held in a struct field, read through `map_get` nested directly inside `unwrap_or`, does not pin K, since the mono pass resolves a field's type only for a generic struct and K no longer appears in `map_get`'s return type for the expected type to pin. The rebind, `slocals: *Map<string, LocalVal> = (*s).locals`, restores the inference; the limitation is recorded in the standard library documentation with the same pattern. A general fix is an inference improvement deferred so this release's compiler stays buildable by the previous release's binary.
- `examples/map_int_keys.dusk` covers an int keyed map end to end, put, get, overwrite, has, remove across a probe chain, length, and insertion order; `examples/map_char_rune_keys.dusk` covers char and rune keys; `examples/map_collision_stress.dusk` drives four hundred keys through multiple grows, removes half, and verifies every survivor; `examples/map_nested_keys.dusk` nests an int keyed map inside a string keyed one; `examples/map_struct_key_reject.dusk` pins the non hashable key reject at the ground type.

Soundness.

- A top-level function named `hash` passed the checker, which resolved a call to it as the user's function, while codegen resolved the same bare name to the builtin unconditionally, so the program computed the builtin's hash where the checker had accepted the user's signature, silently: a two argument `hash` hashed only its first argument, and a function reference `f := hash` called the user function while `hash(v)` beside it called the builtin. The declaration is now refused in the resolver the same way the eight cast names are, `'hash' is a builtin; a function cannot take its name`, with `examples/hash_shadow_fail.dusk` pinning the reject. The spec also now states plainly that a numeric cast is unchecked, `rune(v)` included, which can mint a value no Unicode scalar carries; a consumer that needs a valid scalar checks it itself.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 695 records, 6 new since 1.5.1: five for the generic key surface and the `hash` shadow reject.
- The compiler's own migration is output invisible: the `v1.5.1` release binary and the migrated compiler emit byte identical LLVM IR over the whole example corpus, so the map going generic changed nothing the compiler produces.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.5.1` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (58a3fee4...) and one compiler IR sha256 (654165fb...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 695/695 under stage1 and stage2 alike.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.5.1` release binary builds the 1.5.2 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green.

## 1.5.1

The hash builtin. This is the groundwork for a generic key-value map: a standard library `Map` is keyed only by strings today, and generalizing it over its key type needs one thing the language did not have, a way to hash an arbitrary key. `hash(v)` is that primitive, and it ships one release ahead of the map migration so the previous release's compiler can still build the standard library that will use it. It touches the resolver, the type checker, codegen, and the runtime; no standard library change yet.

The hash builtin.

- `hash(v)` returns a deterministic 64-bit hash of a hashable value: an integer of any width, a `char`, a `rune`, or a `string`. It is a value builtin dispatched by its operand's type the same way `int64(v)` is, added to the builtin sets in `compiler/resolveexpr.dusk`, `compiler/summary.dusk`, and `compiler/monosolve.dusk`, and checked in `compiler/tccall.dusk` through a new `hashable` predicate that mirrors the comparison rule's `comparable` minus float and bool. A float is refused, since a NaN breaks the coherence between a hash and equality; a struct, pointer, slice, and every other non scalar is refused too, `cannot hash <type>; a map key is an integer, char, rune, or string`. In a generic function `hash(k)` passes the surface pass and a non hashable instantiation is rejected on the ground pass, the same two pass shape a generic comparison takes.
- `gen_hash` in `compiler/gencall.dusk` lowers it by the operand's ground type: an integer, char, or rune widens to `i64` through the ordinary coercion, its own value the hash; a string hashes its bytes through a new runtime shim `cool_hash_str` rather than its pointer, so equal content hashes equal and matches the content `==` compares strings by. The shim reproduces the previous pure dusk `map_hash` byte for byte, each byte read unsigned and the accumulator wrapping in `uint64_t`, so a string key will probe to the identical slot once the map goes generic.
- `examples/hash_scalar.dusk` and `examples/hash_string_content.dusk` pin the properties, that equal values and equal string content hash equal and distinct values hash apart, including a multibyte string that exercises the unsigned byte read; the exact hash value is never asserted, since it is unspecified. `examples/mapkv_probe.dusk` builds a whole generic open addressing map on `hash` and generic `==`, exercising an integer key and a string key across a deliberate collision chain, so the map the next release ports into the standard library is proven a release early. `examples/hash_struct_reject.dusk`, `examples/hash_float_reject.dusk`, `examples/hash_generic_ground_reject.dusk`, and `examples/hash_arity_reject.dusk` pin the rejects, the last through a generic instantiation so the ground pass enforcement is covered.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 689 records, 7 new since 1.5.0 for the hash properties, the generic map probe, and the rejects.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.5.0` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (e6041182...) and one compiler IR sha256 (235d32c1...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 689/689 under stage1 and stage2 alike.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.5.0` release binary builds the 1.5.1 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green.

## 1.5.0

The numeric cast. 1.2.0 added an integer width cast, `int8(v)` through `int64(v)` and `char(v)`, but stopped at the integer family: a float could not cross to an integer or the reverse, and `rune` and the float widths were not cast targets at all. This release opens the whole scalar set. `rune(v)`, `float32(v)`, and `float64(v)` join the cast builtins, and a cast now converts across the integer and float boundary in both directions. This is the first release of the 1.5.x line, which continues into a standard library overhaul. It touches the resolver, the type checker, and codegen; no runtime or standard library change.

The numeric cast.

- `cast_target_ty` in `compiler/tccall.dusk` gains `rune`, `float32`, and `float64` as targets, and the operand rule now admits a float source alongside the integer family, so every one of the eight names casts a scalar to its type. A non scalar keeps its reject, reworded for the wider set, `a numeric cast takes an integer, char, rune, or float value; <type> does not cast`. `compiler/resolve.dusk` and `compiler/resolveexpr.dusk` add the three new names to the builtin call set and to the list a user function may not take, since a call to a cast name would otherwise be ambiguous.
- `coerce` in `compiler/genstate.dusk` gains the two conversions it lacked. An integer to a float lowers through `sitofp`, or `uitofp` for a `char` or `bool` source so a magnitude crosses unsigned the same way the integer widening already zero extends those two. A float to an integer lowers through the saturating intrinsic `@llvm.fptosi.sat` or `@llvm.fptoui.sat`, declared on demand: an in range value truncates toward zero exactly as C does, and a magnitude beyond the target's range clamps to its nearest bound while a NaN casts to zero, rather than the poison a bare `fptosi` leaves for those inputs. A misused float to integer cast is therefore deterministic, dusk's safety posture over C's undefined behavior. A float to a float keeps the existing `fpext` and `fptrunc`.
- `examples/cast_numeric.dusk` exercises the matrix, an integer through a float and back, a negative float truncating toward zero, a `float32` widening, an integer to a `rune`, and a `char` crossing unsigned. `examples/cast_saturate.dusk` pins the saturation, an infinity clamping to a bound and a NaN casting to zero. `examples/cast_string_reject.dusk` and `examples/cast_pointer_reject.dusk` pin the non scalar rejects.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 682 records, 4 new since 1.4.4 for the cast matrix, the saturation, and the two rejects; the old integer-only float-reject golden was retired, since a float now casts.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.4.4` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (5d090a25...) and one compiler IR sha256 (6fca0b7c...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 682/682 under stage1 and stage2 alike.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.4.4` release binary builds the 1.5.0 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green.

## 1.4.4

The boundary hardened. The four releases before this one built the C boundary in both directions; this one closes the line by testing the whole of it against adversarial input and fixing what that surfaced. No new language surface: the changes are fault goldens across every boundary feature, four defect fixes in library packaging and the JSON parser, and the interop chapter consolidated in the spec. This release touches the driver, the loader, the standard library, and the test runner; the compiler's front end and codegen are unchanged.

Fault goldens across the boundary.

- `examples/callback_fault_inside.dusk` drives libc's `qsort` with a comparator that indexes out of bounds on its first call: a fault inside a callback body aborts the whole process by name, `fatal: index out of bounds`, the same as anywhere else, proving a callback body carries every runtime guarantee.
- A new `embed_c_fault` test runner builtin builds `examples/embed_fault_lib.dusk` as a C library and drives two faulting exports from a C host: an out of bounds index aborts with the bounds fault, and a `collector<T>` minted inside an export in library mode aborts with `fatal: the collector runs on the main thread only`, since a library has no dusk main to anchor the collected heap. Both prove a dusk fault crosses the export boundary as a clean abort, never corruption.
- The System V x86_64 struct classification was re-checked against `clang`'s own lowering over the shapes most likely to expose an eightbyte error, a twelve byte struct with trailing padding, a nested fixed array crossing an eightbyte, an exactly sixteen byte struct, an integer and SSE mix, and a memory class struct returned and passed by value; dusk's emitted parameter, return, and `byval`/`sret` coercions match `clang -O0` on every one. The variadic default argument promotions were re-verified end to end, an `int8`, a `char`, and a `float32` on the vararg tail read back correctly through libc `printf`.

Library packaging.

- A library's private symbols could interpose the host's own libc at the static link. A private dusk helper, or a `@csource`'s own function, named for a libc entry such as `read` or `rand` was emitted as a global symbol in the archive, so a host that linked the archive and called that libc function was silently redirected into dusk. The module object and every `@csource` object now merge into one relocatable object with `ld -r`, and `llvm-objcopy` then keeps only the exports global and makes every other symbol local, so nothing but an export is visible to the host. A build with no exports at all localizes every symbol through a sentinel keep list, rather than leaving them global. A `@csource`'s functions are the library's implementation, not its interface, so a host can no longer call one directly; that is intended, and it fails at link with an undefined reference rather than silently.
- Two archive objects that shared a basename collided on disk before `llvm-ar` ran, dropping the runtime or the module from the archive; every object now carries a class prefix and an index. A `@csource` referenced by two files under different spellings, `"impl.c"` and `"./impl.c"`, compiled the same file twice into duplicate symbols; the loader now cleans a resolved `@csource` path lexically, dropping `.` and resolving `..` segments, before it deduplicates, so one file compiles once however it is spelled.

std.json hardening.

- A deeply nested document overflowed the stack: `json_parse` recurses once per nesting level, and a pathological input of many thousand open brackets crashed the process. The parser now bounds nesting at a fixed depth well above any real document and returns a `nesting is too deep` error past it, so a malicious input is a clean error rather than a crash.
- A grammatically valid but overflowing number, `1e400` among them, parsed to an infinity that `json_emit` then wrote as `inf`, text that is not JSON and does not reparse. `json_parse` now refuses a number whose magnitude overflows a `float64` with a `number out of range` error, keeping the accepted set inside real, round-trippable JSON.

Known limitations, recorded.

- A parsed `Json` tree is a set of managed heap allocations with no `json_free` in this release: a value read out of a `match` arm is a borrow, so a recursive walk cannot free a tree it does not own, and a tree is reclaimed at process exit rather than by an explicit call. A long running program that parses many documents holds them until it exits.
- The library archive is static only. Its objects are not position independent and the runtime's thread local storage takes the local exec model, so the archive does not link into a shared object a `dlopen` based FFI like Python's `ctypes` loads directly; a position independent shared object build is left to a later release.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 679 records, 4 new since 1.4.3: the callback fault, the embedding fault, the deep nesting reject, and the number overflow reject.
- The stage ladder re-fixed with the `v1.4.3` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (9792ddf0...) and one compiler IR sha256 (5b8456c9...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 679/679 under stage1 and stage2 alike.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.4.3` release binary builds the 1.4.4 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green.

## 1.4.3

Dusk as a library. The four releases before this one carried C into dusk, a variadic call, a linked library, a struct by value, a callback. This one carries dusk out. An `export "C"` function is a dusk function a C caller reaches directly, by its own bare symbol, and `dusk build --lib` compiles a whole module into a static archive and a generated C header any language with a C FFI links against. One standard library module lands on top, `std.json`, a parser and emitter over a recursive enum, pure dusk but for a single number formatting shim. This release touches the parser, sema, codegen, the driver, the CLI, and the C runtime; every stage from parse to link gains a small piece of the export path.

Exporting to C.

- `compiler/parseitem.dusk` reads a calling convention string after `export`: `export "C" func` marks a function a C ABI entry point, threading an `abi_c` flag onto `FuncNode` (a new `abi_export` field carried through `compiler/desugar.dusk`, `compiler/monorw.dusk`, and `compiler/loadfold.dusk`, the three places a function is copied). A non-`"C"` convention string is rejected, `the only supported export ABI is "C"`, and `export "C"` before an `async func`, a struct, an enum, an interface, or anything but a function is rejected in place.
- `check_export_sig` in `compiler/tcrun.dusk` gates the surface: an exported function may not be generic, may not be named `main` or the entry wrapper's own `dusk__main`, and may not begin with `cool_`, the runtime's reserved prefix. Every parameter and the return must be a scalar, a `*raw T`, or a `*void`, with `void` allowed as the return; a struct by value on an export is deferred and rejected by name, as is a string, slice, managed pointer, closure, interface, or error. `bool` is rejected specifically, `use char or int8 at the boundary`, since a dusk `bool` lowers to an `i1` no C header type spells without a byte against bit mismatch.
- Codegen emits an export by its exact name: privatization skips it, so its symbol is never suffixed, and monomorphization already copies every non-generic function and walks its body, so an export reached from no dusk call at all still lands in the module, and a generic function reached only through an export is still instantiated. `compiler/genfn.dusk` gives every export a `cool_lib_init` prologue, an idempotent `pthread_once` hook in `runtime/runtime.c` that exists so future load time setup never changes the exported ABI.

Building a library.

- `dusk build --lib <file>` in `compiler/dusk.dusk` and `compiler/driver.dusk` compiles a module to a linkable library rather than an executable. `build_lib` writes `stem.ll`, then `archive_lib` compiles it and every runtime source and every `@csource` to objects and bundles them with `llvm-ar` in its deterministic mode into `lib<stem>.a`, a self contained archive a host links and needs nothing else of dusk's besides. `write_lib_header` generates `stem.h` beside it: `#include <stdint.h>`, one prototype per export inside an `extern "C"` guard, each prototype's C types lowered from the same source the emitted symbol is, so the header can never drift from the archive. The module may omit `main` entirely.
- The export prototypes travel from codegen out through `GenResult` and `CliIrResult`, collected in `compiler/gen.dusk` as the module lowers, so the header is a pure function of the exports the same way the archive is.
- `examples/embed_lib.dusk` and `examples/embed_host.c`, driven by a new `embed_c_lib` builtin in the test runner, prove the whole path end to end: build the library, link a C host against the archive, run it, and assert its output. `examples/export_c_ok.dusk` proves an export compiles inside a normal binary, and seven reject twins pin the generic, `main`, `cool_`, `dusk__main`, bool, struct parameter, and bad ABI string rejects.

`std.json`, a new module.

- `Json` is a recursive enum, `JNull`, `JBool`, `JNum` over a `float64`, `JStr`, `JArr` over a `*Vector<*Json>`, and `JObj` over a `*Map<*Json>`. `json_parse(s: string) -> (*Json, error)` parses a full JSON document, every value kind, nested arrays and objects, insignificant whitespace, numbers with a sign, fraction, and exponent, and string escapes including `\uXXXX` and a surrogate pair decoded to UTF-8 through `std.unicode`; a malformed document returns a named error rather than a value. `json_emit(j: *Json) -> string` renders a value back to compact JSON, re-escaping strings and formatting a number through `cool_f64_str`, a new `%.17g` runtime shim that round trips a `float64` through decimal.
- `examples/json_roundtrip.dusk` parses a document with every value kind and a `\u` escape and emits it back exactly; `examples/json_number_roundtrip.dusk` proves `json_parse(json_emit(x))` recovers the same `float64` over a value table; `examples/json_malformed.dusk` walks a set of malformed inputs, each returning its own error and none crashing; `examples/json_type_reject.dusk` pins a wrong payload type at the enum constructor.

Soundness.

- An exported `bool` parameter or return crossed as an LLVM `i1` while the generated header declared it `uint8_t`: a C caller passing a byte with any high bit set, `true` by every C convention, was read by the callee as bit 0 only, so a value of 2 took the false branch. `check_export_sig` now rejects `bool` at an export boundary outright, directing the boundary to `char` or `int8`, which cross as a full byte.
- A library's private helper kept external linkage in the archive, so a private dusk function named for a libc entry, `random`, `read`, or `write` among them, interposed the host's own libc call at the static link and silently redirected it into dusk. `archive_lib` now runs `llvm-objcopy` over the module object before archiving, keeping only the exports global and localizing every other symbol, so a private name can never satisfy a host's reference. This class was newly reachable through `--lib`, since an executable build owned its whole link.
- An export named `dusk__main` passed the name check, which refused only `main`, and codegen's entry special case matched the mangled name too and gave it the collector anchor prologue. A host calling it once anchored the collector at a transient frame that then returned, defeating the library mode fault that otherwise aborts a collection with no dusk main; a later collection could sweep a still live block. `check_export_sig` now rejects `dusk__main` as well, and the anchor prologue in `compiler/genfn.dusk` is guarded to never fire for an exported function.
- Two archive objects that shared a basename collided on disk before `llvm-ar` ran: a `@csource "runtime.c"`, or a library source named `runtime.dusk`, overwrote the runtime's own `runtime.o` and dropped the dusk core from the archive. Every object now carries a class prefix and an index, `duskmod_`, `duskrt_`, and `duskcs_`, so no two objects in an archive ever share a name.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 675 records, 13 new since 1.4.2: the embedding golden, the export normal binary golden, seven export reject twins, and the four json goldens.
- The stage ladder re-fixed on the new surface with the `v1.4.2` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (ef06ad53...) and one compiler IR sha256 (f35d66c6...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 675/675 under stage1 and stage2 alike.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.4.2` release binary builds the 1.4.3 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green.
- Four export defects were found and closed before the cut, each with a golden or a verified repro: a `bool` export that read a caller's byte as one bit, a private helper that interposed the host's libc, an export named `dusk__main` that anchored the collector at a returning frame, and an archive object name collision that dropped the runtime.
- The archive is static only in this release. Its objects are not position independent and the runtime's thread local storage takes the local exec model, so the archive does not link into a shared object a dlopen based FFI like Python's ctypes loads directly; a position independent shared object build is left to later work.

## 1.4.2

The callback. 1.4.1 opened the foreign boundary to a struct crossing by value; 1.4.2 opens it to the shape most C libraries actually ask for at their own call sites, a callback. A `foreign` function's parameter may now be declared with a function type, and a dusk function, a capture-free lambda literal or the name of a top level function, may stand in that position and cross as a bare C function pointer, one LLVM word with no environment and no trampoline in between, exactly the shape `qsort`'s comparator or a library's own registration call expects. Two standard library pieces land on top of the reopened boundary: `std.process`, a new module that runs a shell command as a child process and reads its output back line by line, and two additions to `std.vector`, `vec_sort`, a stable, deterministic in place sort behind a comparator closure, and the `vec_contains`/`vec_index_of` pair. This release touches sema's foreign checks, codegen's call and closure lowering, the C runtime, and the standard library; no other pipeline stage changes.

Callbacks at the C boundary.

- `foreign_ty_ok` in `compiler/tciface.dusk` gains a `tyk_func` arm: a function type classifies as boundary legal when every parameter and the return is itself a scalar or a raw pointer, refusing a struct by value in a callback signature and refusing a function type nested inside one, `foreign function '<name>': a C callback's own parameters and return must be scalars or raw pointers; a nested function type cannot cross`. `check_foreign` refuses a foreign function that returns a function pointer outright, `foreign function '<name>' cannot return a function pointer; a returned C function pointer has no dusk value to call, only an argument callback is supported`, and refuses combining a callback parameter with a variadic tail, `foreign function '<name>' is variadic and cannot take a function pointer parameter; a C callback and a varargs tail cannot be combined`.
- `check_callback_arg` in `compiler/tccall.dusk` is the call site check, dispatched from `infer_call` whenever a foreign callee's declared parameter is a function type. It accepts a capture-free lambda literal or the bare name of a top level, non-generic, non-foreign dusk function, checked against the callback's own parameter type by `ty_strict_eq`, a new structural equality in `compiler/ty.dusk` with no integer width widening and no wildcarding, so a callback binds only to a function whose ABI is identical. A captured local, a signature mismatch, a generic function name, or any other shape in that position is refused by a message naming the cause: `this callback captures '<var>'; C has no environment for it, pass state through the *void user data argument the API carries`, `callback argument N: expected <sig>, found <sig>`, `callback argument N cannot be the generic function '<name>'; a C callback needs one concrete function, and a generic function has none until it is instantiated`, and `callback argument N must be a capture-free lambda literal or the name of a top-level function; a closure value has no bare C function pointer`.
- `foreign_takes_callback` in `compiler/tcexpr.dusk` closes the value position: a foreign function whose signature carries a callback parameter or return cannot itself be taken as a value, `'<name>' is a foreign function that takes a C callback; call it directly, it cannot be taken as a value`, the same family `sig_is_variadic_foreign` and `foreign_takes_struct` already refuse there, since a funcval thunk would pass the callback as a closure and drop the bare pointer ABI a direct call preserves.
- `compiler/cty.dusk` adds `ctyk_cfnptr`, a bare pointer C type distinct from a closure's two word `{ ptr, ptr }`, and `cty_lower_foreign` lowers a foreign declaration's own parameter and return types through it in place of the ordinary closure lowering. `compiler/genclos.dusk`'s `gen_cb_lambda` lifts a capture-free lambda to a genuine top level function with no `%env` parameter at all, generalizing the existing lambda emitter (`clos_emit_lambda_ex`) to skip it. `compiler/gencall.dusk`'s `gc_callback_operand` resolves a callback argument, lambda or named function, to its bare `@name` address with no forwarding thunk, wired into both the struct carrying call path and the ordinary one.
- `examples/callback_qsort.dusk` and `examples/callback_named_fn.dusk` drive libc's `qsort` with a lambda and a named comparator; `examples/callback_fixture_repeat.dusk`, paired with a new `@csource` fixture `examples/callback_fixture.c`, round trips a `*void` user data argument through several callback invocations. `examples/callback_capture_reject.dusk`, `examples/callback_sig_reject.dusk`, and `examples/callback_generic_fn_reject.dusk` pin three of the call site rejects.

`std.process`, a new module.

- A `foreign` block binds three new C runtime shims, `cool_popen`, `cool_fgets`, and `cool_pclose`, rather than `popen`/`fgets`/`pclose` directly, since a `FILE*` stream cannot ride home as a `*void` a dusk wrapper could NULL test: `==` on any pointer type is rejected outright, the same constraint `std.fs`'s `Dir` carries for `DIR*`. `Proc` wraps its stream the identical way, one `int64` field holding the pointer's bit pattern.
- `proc_open(cmd: string) -> (Proc, error)` runs `cmd` through the platform shell's own `popen(cmd, "r")` and opens a readable pipe to its combined stdout. `proc_read_line(p: Proc) -> (string, bool)` reads the next line without its trailing newline, reassembling across as many `cool_fgets` calls as it takes when a line outruns the internal 4096 byte chunk, so no line length silently truncates; the second value is `false`, with the empty string first, once the stream is exhausted or a hard read error occurs. `proc_close(p: Proc) -> (int64, error)` closes the stream and reaps the child, decoding `pclose`'s wait status the same way `std.os`'s `run` decodes `system`'s: the low 7 bits name a terminating signal, reported as 128 plus the signal number, and a normal exit reports the shifted down exit code. `run_capture(cmd: string) -> (string, int64, error)` is the convenience wrapper: open, read every line into one newline joined string, close, hand back the output alongside the decoded exit code.
- `examples/proc_capture.dusk` runs `run_capture` against an `echo` and a `sh -c` command that exits non zero, proving the wait status decode against real output rather than only the common `exit(0)` case; `examples/process_run_capture_type_reject.dusk` pins `run_capture`'s `cmd` parameter as a string with no implicit coercion from an int literal.

`std.vector`: sort and search.

- `vec_sort<T>(v: *Vector<T>, cmp: (T, T) -> int32) -> void` is a bottom up, iterative merge sort: passes double a merged run width from 1 up past `v`'s length, each pass merging adjacent runs out of `v`'s backing data into one scratch buffer sized to `v` through `vec_sort_merge_run`, then copying the whole buffer back before the next pass starts. The sort is stable, a tie (`cmp` returning 0) always favors the earlier run, and deterministic: two calls over the same data and `cmp` always produce the same output order. A vector of fewer than two elements returns immediately with `cmp` uncalled.
- `vec_contains<T>(v: *Vector<T>, x: T) -> bool` and `vec_index_of<T>(v: *Vector<T>, x: T) -> int64` are a linear scan by `==`, legal only for a `T` that `==` itself accepts, a scalar or a string, never a pointer, dusk's own comparison rule; a `Vector<T>` of a pointer typed `T` rejects both at the ground types the same way any other illegal `==` does.
- `examples/vec_sort_stable.dusk` pushes eight `(key, orig_index)` pairs in a scrambled key order and proves stability: every run of equal keys keeps its indices in push order after the sort. `examples/vec_sort_arity_reject.dusk` pins `vec_sort`'s arity at exactly two arguments, the vector pointer and the comparator, with no silently ignored extra.

Soundness.

- A foreign function that itself takes a C callback, taken as a value rather than called directly, skipped straight past the ABI: the value position resolves through an ordinary funcval thunk that passes a callback as a `{ ptr, ptr }` env-and-function-pointer closure, where C reads a single function pointer word, the same miscompile class 1.4.1 closed for a variadic foreign function and a struct passing one. `foreign_takes_callback` in `compiler/tcexpr.dusk` closes it for a callback taking foreign function too.
- A callback whose own declared signature nested a further function type in a parameter or its return classified as boundary legal by the `tyk_func` arm's plain recursion, when C carries a callback as exactly one function pointer word with no room underneath it for a nested closure's second word. `callback_has_nested_fn` and `foreign_param_reject` in `compiler/tciface.dusk` refuse the combination by name, and `foreign_ty_ok`'s own `tyk_func` arm now refuses the same nesting directly rather than recursing into it.
- `cool_fgets` in `runtime/runtime.c` casts a dusk `int64` cap explicitly to the plain `int` libc's `fgets` itself takes before the call, rather than letting the wider argument stand in unconverted for the narrower C parameter.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 662 records, 21 new since 1.4.1: the qsort callback through a lambda and a named function, the callback fixture with user data through the void pointer, the process capture, the stable sort, and the boundary and capture reject twins.
- The stage ladder re-fixed on the new surface with the `v1.4.1` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (a4bfb7b4...) and one compiler IR sha256 (5fab8452...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 662/662 under stage1 and stage2 alike.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.4.1` release binary builds the 1.4.2 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green.
- Five callback and process defects were found and closed before the cut, each with a golden: a callback taken as a value and a nested function type in a callback signature, both of which slipped past the checker and corrupted at runtime, a capture analysis that let a nested scope's binding mask an outer capture so a capturing callback was lifted with no environment, a range capture that read the wrong endpoints, and a process handle that could be closed twice into libc.

## 1.4.1

Structs across. 1.4.0 opened the foreign boundary to a variadic call and a linker directive; 1.4.1 opens it to a value the boundary had refused outright since 0.2.4, a named struct passed or returned by value. A `foreign` declaration may now take or return a struct whose fields are all C plain, classified and coerced the way clang's own System V x86_64 ABI places it, eightbyte by eightbyte, register or memory, byte exact against a clang compiled object on either side of the call. Two standard library modules land on top of the reopened boundary: `std.fs`, file and directory operations plus pure dusk path arithmetic, and `std.time`, wall clock reads paired with a pure dusk proleptic Gregorian calendar, both UTC only. This release touches sema's foreign checks, codegen's call and declare lowering, the C runtime, and the standard library; no other pipeline stage changes.

Struct by value at the C boundary.

- `compiler/cabi.dusk` is a new file, the System V x86_64 struct classifier: `cabi_flatten` walks a C plain type into scalar leaves at absolute byte offsets, recursing through a nested struct and a fixed array; `cabi_eightbyte_sse` and `cabi_int_ll` decide, eightbyte by eightbyte, between an SSE register and an INTEGER register coerced to the narrowest type that covers the data in it, `cabi_bits_no_user_data` reproducing clang's own `BitsContainNoUserData` test that lets a partial eightbyte stay narrow instead of widening to `i64`. `cabi_classify` is the entry point: a struct over sixteen bytes classifies to memory, `byval` going in and `sret` coming back, `cabi_byval_align` raising a memory argument's alignment to the eightbyte floor a stack slot needs; everything at or under sixteen bytes classifies to one or two coerced registers.
- Sema gates what may reach the classifier at all. `foreign_struct_cplain` and `foreign_cplain_field` in `compiler/tciface.dusk` walk a struct's fields and reject the first one that is not C plain, by name: `bool` (`field '<name>' is bool; use char or int8 at the boundary`), `float32` (`field '<name>' is float32; use float64 or pass by pointer`), a managed pointer, a string, a slice, a tuple, a closure, a collected value, a future, an error, a thread handle, an interface, an enum, or a generic struct, each with its own message. `foreign_param_reject` folds a struct's own classification into the ordinary foreign parameter and return check, and a variadic foreign function rejects a struct outright at either position, fixed or vararg, `variadic_struct_decl_msg`, since C reads a vararg positionally with nothing to classify a struct against.
- Codegen lowers both directions of a struct-carrying call. `compiler/gen.dusk`'s `foreign_declare` emits the `declare` line for a foreign function, coercing a struct parameter or return to its `sret`/`byval` memory form or its coerced register form so the declaration matches what a C compiler gave the definition it links against. `compiler/gencall.dusk`'s `gen_foreign_struct_call` lowers a call: a struct argument is built into an alloca and passed `byval` or split into its coerced register pieces, `fscall_push_pieces`, and a struct return is read back out of an `sret` slot or reassembled from its register pieces into one. `fscall_needs_abi` decides, per call, whether a signature carries a struct at all and needs this path or the ordinary scalar one.
- The classification is System V x86_64 only; `x86_64-pc-linux-gnu` is the only target triple `compiler/gen.dusk` emits, so struct by value stands on the same single-target footing every other codegen decision already does.
- Eight new goldens pin the ABI: `examples/cabi_small_int.dusk`, `cabi_small_sse.dusk`, and `cabi_small_mixed.dusk` cover a one and two eightbyte struct under every INTEGER/SSE combination, each checked against a matching `.c` file compiled in through `@csource`; `cabi_return_pair.dusk` and `cabi_return_sret.dusk` cover a register pair return and a memory return round tripping back through a `byval` parameter; `cabi_memory_16plus.dusk` pins the sixteen byte cutover into memory. `cabi_bool_reject.dusk`, `cabi_float32_reject.dusk`, `cabi_slice_field_reject.dusk`, and `cabi_variadic_struct_reject.dusk` pin four of the named field and variadic rejects.

`std.fs`, a new module.

- A `foreign` block binds `open`, `close`, `read`, `write`, `lseek`, `mkdir`, `rmdir`, `unlink`, and `rename` directly, `open` variadic to carry an optional create mode; `open_file`, `close_file`, `read_bytes`, `write_bytes`, `seek`, `make_dir`, `remove_dir`, `remove_file`, and `move_file` wrap each with `std.os`'s errno convention, reading `errno()` immediately after the call it names and returning an `error` built from `errstr` on failure.
- `file_stat(path: string) -> (FileStat, error)` and directory iteration go through two new C runtime shims instead of a direct binding, since `struct stat` and `DIR` are C layouts dusk never reads. `cool_stat` in `runtime/runtime.c` fills three plain `int64` out parameters, size, mode, and mtime, rather than handing back a struct; `cool_dir_open`, `cool_dir_next`, and `cool_dir_close` move an opened stream through `Dir`, a struct wrapping one `int64` handle field, `dir_open`, `dir_next`, and `dir_close` on the dusk side.
- `path_join`, `path_dirname`, `path_basename`, and `path_extension` are pure dusk path arithmetic, ported from the same logic the compiler's own driver and home modules use to lay out `target/dusk-out` and resolve `DUSK_HOME`, reproduced here so `std.fs` carries no dependency on the compiler.
- Two shapes of the boundary surfaced while writing this module, both real constraints rather than implementation accidents. First, dusk gives every exported top level function a bare, unmangled link symbol, and libc already owns `stat(2)`, `dirname(3)`, and `basename(3)` among the names this module would otherwise reach for; an export named `stat` would resolve the module's own internal calls to itself instead of libc's `stat`, so the module exports `file_stat`, `path_dirname`, and `path_basename` in the collision's place. An export is not yet checked against what the binary already links, so a later module choosing a colliding name would trip the same silent redirect with no diagnostic; that check is left to later work. Second, `==` on any pointer type is rejected by the language itself, `pointers do not compare; compare the values they point to`, so a directory stream cannot ride home as a `*void` the way a C caller null checks `opendir`'s own return; `Dir.h` carries it as a bit pattern `int64` instead, and every call on it that can fail reports failure through a separate status value rather than a null check dusk has no way to write.
- `examples/fs_roundtrip.dusk` opens, writes, seeks, reads back, stats, and removes a file, then walks a directory with `dir_open`/`dir_next`/`dir_close`; `examples/fs_open_missing.dusk` and `examples/fs_stat_arg_fail.dusk` pin the error path on a missing file.

`std.time`, a new module.

- `now_ns`, `now_ms`, and `now_unix` read nanoseconds, milliseconds, and whole seconds since the Unix epoch, UTC. `now_ns` is the one foreign call, `cool_unix_now_ns` in `runtime/runtime.c`, a shim over `clock_gettime(CLOCK_REALTIME)`; `now_ms` and `now_unix` are pure arithmetic over it.
- `civil_from_unix(secs: int64) -> Civil` and `unix_from_civil(c: Civil) -> int64` convert between a Unix timestamp and a `Civil` value, year/month/day/hour/minute/second, through `days_from_civil` and `civil_from_days`, Howard Hinnant's proleptic Gregorian calendar arithmetic ported to dusk, correct with no lookup table and exact through at least year 2400 in both directions, checked here against 200,000 random dates before this module shipped. `floor_div` and `floor_mod` give the calendar arithmetic floor semantics over dusk's own truncating `/` and `%`, so a timestamp before the epoch lands the quotient one lower rather than snapping toward zero.
- `format_iso8601(c: Civil) -> string` renders `Civil` as `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SSZ`, the trailing `Z` marking UTC since the module carries no other zone; there is no time zone support at all, every value here is UTC and a caller wanting local time converts outside the module.
- `examples/time_civil.dusk` round trips a handful of known timestamps through `civil_from_unix`, `unix_from_civil`, and `format_iso8601`; `examples/time_civil_arg_fail.dusk` pins an out of range field rolling forward through the day count arithmetic rather than faulting.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 645 records, 19 new since 1.4.0: the struct by value round trips across every SysV shape class, the register exhaustion and variadic struct return cases, the filesystem round trip, the civil calendar round trip, and the boundary reject twins.
- The stage ladder re-fixed on the new surface with the `v1.4.0` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (c2ada755...) and one compiler IR sha256 (423465d0...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 645/645 under stage1 and stage2 alike.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.4.0` release binary builds the 1.4.1 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green.
- Six struct boundary defects were found and closed before the cut, each with a golden and checked byte for byte against clang: a register class struct that must fall to the stack once the argument registers are spent but was split into pieces, a struct carrying foreign function taken as a value that skipped the ABI, a fieldless struct that shifted every following argument, a variadic struct return the checker wrongly refused and then miscompiled once accepted, a byval slot whose alignment claim outran the slot, and a bounded per classification leak.

## 1.4.0

The open boundary. Every foreign function since 0.2.4 crossed on the raw pointer layer only, one fixed signature per C symbol, and the boundary itself could only ever bind against whatever the runtime and libc happened to already link; 1.4.0 opens it on three sides at once. A `foreign` block can now declare a variadic C function, checked at its fixed arity and promoted at its tail the way a C compiler promotes its own variadic call sites. Two new directives, `@link` and `@csource`, reach the link line itself, so a program can pull in a third party library or compile a C file in beside the runtime with no change to the build. And `std.os` gets the read side of the C library's own error channel, `errno` and `errstr`. Two standard library modules ship alongside the boundary work: `std.math`, libm's scalar functions bound straight across it, and `std.rand`, an xoshiro256** generator seeded through splitmix64. One correction rides with the release: float `!=` now answers IEEE 754's unordered comparison rather than the ordered one every release through 1.3.1 shipped, so a NaN operand makes it `true` instead of `false`. This release touches the prescan line sweep, the parser, sema's call checking, codegen's call lowering, the standard library, and the C runtime; no other pipeline stage changes.

Variadic foreign functions.

- A `foreign` block's parameter list may end in a bare `...`, parsed by `parse_foreign_params` in `compiler/parsety.dusk`, which marks the whole function variadic and rejects `...` anywhere but the last position, `'...' must be the last parameter of a variadic foreign function`. Only a `foreign` declaration can carry the marker; the ordinary `parse_params` path a ground dusk function's parameter list takes never accepts it, so a plain dusk function still cannot be variadic.
- A call to a variadic foreign function checks in two halves, `check_variadic_call` in `compiler/tccall.dusk`: the fixed parameters check against their declared types exactly as an ordinary call's arguments do, and the call must supply at least that many, `expected at least N argument(s), found M` when it falls short. Every argument beyond the fixed count, the vararg tail, is checked against the same admitted set the foreign boundary already draws: a scalar (an integer of any width, `float32`, `float64`, `bool`, `char`, `rune`), a `*raw T`, or a `*void` rides across; a managed pointer, a string, a slice, an array, a tuple, a struct, a closure, a collected value, a future, an interface value, or an error is rejected by name and argument position, `argument N to a variadic foreign function is <kind>`, each paired with the fix: `pass a *raw T or *void, since a managed *T carries a generation C cannot read`, `pass a *raw char, a string view cannot cross a C vararg directly`, `pass its backing as a *raw T`, `pass its elements individually`, `pass a pointer to it`, or a flat `a variadic foreign function takes only scalars and raw pointers` for the kinds with no crossing at all.
- Codegen lowers a variadic call through `gen_variadic_foreign_call` in `compiler/gencall.dusk`, which builds the call instruction's full LLVM function type, the fixed parameter types followed by a trailing `...`, LLVM's own requirement for a varargs callee. A fixed argument adapts to its declared parameter type the normal way; an argument beyond them takes the C default argument promotions first, `variadic_promote`: `int8` and `int16` sign extend to `int32`, `char` and `bool` zero extend to `int32`, `float32` widens to `float64`, and every other scalar or pointer crosses unchanged, the identical promotion set a C compiler applies at its own variadic call sites.
- `examples/varargs_printf.dusk` and `examples/varargs_snprintf.dusk` call `printf` and `snprintf` across the boundary with an `int64`, a `float64`, and a `*raw char` riding the vararg tail; `examples/varargs_arity_reject.dusk`, `examples/varargs_managed_reject.dusk`, and `examples/varargs_string_reject.dusk` pin the arity shortfall and two of the named argument rejects.

`@link` and `@csource`.

- `@link <value>` and `@csource "<path>"` are two new directives, collected by `prescan` in `compiler/prescan.dusk` alongside `@paradigm` and `@import`, the one line sweep that runs ahead of the lexer. `loader.dusk`'s `collect_directives` folds every file's directives into one module wide, order preserving, deduplicated pair of lists: a `@link` value is carried verbatim since the link step decides between a library flag and a file argument, and a `@csource` path resolves against its declaring file's own directory at load time, so the path the linker receives is already anchored to the invocation root regardless of which file named it.
- `driver.dusk`'s `link_only` builds the `clang` line from that pair: every runtime source first, then each `@csource` file, then each `@link` value, `push_link` deciding per value between a verbatim file argument (a value with a path separator, or ending `.a`, `.o`, or `.so`) and a `-l` flag (everything else), then the fixed `-pthread -lm` and the output path. The whole line is a pure function of the two lists; nothing a directive names becomes an arbitrary flag.
- `examples/link_m.dusk` links `-lm` explicitly and calls `sqrt` across the boundary to prove it; `examples/csource_adder.dusk` compiles `examples/csource_adder.c` in beside the runtime and calls the `add` it defines through a matching `foreign` declaration.

`std.math` and `std.rand`, two new modules.

- `std.math` binds 22 of libm's scalar `float64` functions straight across the foreign boundary with no C shim of its own, `sin` through `fmax`, since libm already ships beside libc and every dusk binary already links `-lm`. `pi` and `e` are plain dusk literals, the two constants libm keeps as macros rather than symbols; `is_nan` and `is_inf` are pure dusk over IEEE 754's own algebra, `!(x == x)` and `x == x && x + x == x && x != 0.0` respectively, needing no foreign call at all.
- `std.rand` is an xoshiro256** generator, D. Blackman and S. Vigna's design, over a heap allocated `Rng` a caller mints with `rng_new(seed)` and frees with `free`. The seed expands into the generator's four state words through splitmix64 first, so a thin seed like 0 or 1 does not leave xoshiro sitting near the all zero fixed point it would otherwise need many draws to mix away. `rng_next` draws a raw 64 bit word; `rng_range(r, lo, hi)` and `rng_float(r)` narrow a draw to a bounded integer and a `[0, 1)` float, and `shuffle(r, xs)` runs Fisher-Yates in place over an `int64[]`. `rng_seed_os()` folds eight bytes of kernel entropy from `getrandom` into a seed for a caller that wants one, without wiring true randomness into the deterministic generator itself. `lshr64` recovers the zero filling shift xoshiro's rotation needs, since dusk's own `>>` sign extends rather than zero fills.
- `examples/math_values.dusk` checks every `std.math` function through a bool comparison against a known value rather than printing a raw `float64`, whose text format is not pinned across platforms; `examples/rand_fixed_seed.dusk` draws five words from a fixed seed and pins them against a reference splitmix64/xoshiro256** implementation.

`std.os` gains `errno` and `errstr`; `std.string` gains `cbuf`.

- `errno() -> int64` in `std.os` reads the C library's errno right back through a new foreign shim, `cool_errno` in `runtime/runtime.c`. dusk itself never sets errno, so a read always reports whatever the most recent foreign call left there, and errno is thread local under the pthreads runtime dusk links, so a read never races another thread's own foreign call, only the calling thread's own next one. `errstr(code: int64) -> string` is `strerror`'s message for a code, copied off `strerror`'s own static buffer into a fresh heap string before the buffer can be overwritten by a later call. `examples/errno_read.dusk` checks the shape of a few `errstr` results, a non empty message, rather than pinning exact wording, since glibc's own text for a code is not fixed across platforms.
- `std.string` gains `cbuf(s: string) -> *raw char`, copying a string into a fresh NUL terminated raw buffer for a foreign call to read. It replaces the private `to_cbuf` helper every foreign consuming example and stdlib module used to carry its own copy of; `std.os`'s `run` and `env` now build their buffers through it instead.

Float `!=` is unordered.

- `!=` between two floats now lowers to LLVM's `une` predicate, unordered not equal, in place of `one`, ordered not equal, `gx_cmp_pred` in `compiler/genexpr.dusk`. IEEE 754 defines `!=` as the negation of `==` at every input, so `NaN != x` must answer `true` for any `x`, NaN included; the ordered form every release through 1.3.1 shipped instead answered `false` on a NaN operand, agreeing with `==` rather than negating it. `==` and the ordering operators, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`, stay ordered, answering `false` against a NaN operand on either side, which was already correct and is unchanged. `examples/nanne.dusk` divides `0.0` by itself to mint a NaN and checks both `!=` and `==` against it.

Numbers.

- `testrun tests/goldens.manifest` passes all 626 records, 17 new since 1.3.1: the variadic printf and snprintf runs, the csource and link builds, the math, rand, and errno runs, the unordered NaN run, and the seven boundary and shadow reject twins.
- The stage ladder re-fixed on the new surface with the `v1.3.1` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (97c4df21...) and one compiler IR sha256 (03321c2f...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, 626/626 under stage1 and stage2 alike.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.3.1` release binary builds the 1.4.0 source and the freshly built compiler passes the full suite. dawn stays byte compatible, 10 of 10 offline checks green.
- Five boundary defects were found and closed before the cut, each with a golden: a variadic dispatch that skipped a shadowing local's own arity check, a clang option injection through a `@link` or `@csource` value opening with a dash, a variadic foreign taken as a first class value that would have dropped the vararg call type, prescan directive faults that reached the scan dump but not a build, and a modulo bias in `rng_range`.

## 1.3.1

The retirement. 1.3.0 built a golden harness in dusk and proved it against the Rust one while both existed; 1.3.1 removes the Rust implementation from the repository. `src/`, `Cargo.toml`, `Cargo.lock`, `tests/examples.rs`, `tests/parser_termination.rs`, and the Rust-only fixture corpora are deleted, and the compiler written in dusk is no longer just canonical but alone. The Rust tree lives on at [dusk-rust](https://github.com/choice404/dusk-rust), a frozen full history archive of this repository at `v1.3.0`, the reference implementation of the language surface through 1.2.0. No language surface changes and no compiler behavior changes beyond the version line and message prefixes; the diff is dominated by deletion.

The excision.

- `src/` and both Cargo files are gone, along with the Rust test estate: `tests/examples.rs` (the golden suite now lives entirely in `tests/goldens.manifest`), `tests/parser_termination.rs` (its corpus was harvested into `examples/parseterm_*.dusk` at 1.3.0, run under the runner's timeout), the `tests/fixtures/` and `tests/sema_corpus/` trees that only the Rust unit tests and the bootstrap-era differential drove, and `tools/sema_manifest.sh`. git history is untouched: every tag, `src/` included, remains reachable here forever.
- The compiler root is renamed `compiler/main.dusk` to `compiler/dusk.dusk`, so a self build emits `target/dusk-out/dusk` under the driver's name-by-stem rule. A file rename shifts the per file suffixes `privatize` mints, which moves the compiler's own IR; the stage ladder below re-fixes the fixpoint on the new bytes.
- The CLI messages drop the bootstrap era name: `usage: dusk <command> [file]`, `dusk: unknown command`, and the rest now carry the `dusk:` prefix, and `tools/pyramid.sh`'s build probe accepts either prefix so the ladder can still drive older stages.
- `tools/dawn-smoke.sh` builds dawn with the dusk compiler and keeps its eleven offline checks; the section that diffed against the Rust dawn is gone with the Rust dawn itself. `tools/testrun-selftest.sh` defaults its compiler to `target/dusk-out/dusk`.

The archive.

- `dusk-rust` is a full history clone at `v1.3.0` plus one commit replacing the README. Full history means the `v1.0.0` seed pin is inside it, so the archive alone suffices for the from-Rust audit bootstrap. The tree carries the complete frozen pair, `src/` and `compiler/` at the same tag, so the strongest differential the language has, two independent compilers over one corpus, still runs there in one command.
- Proven standing alone before the cut: in a fresh clone, `cargo build --release` and the full `cargo test` pass, and a `tools/differential.sh` parse run over the corpus against the `v1.3.0` dusk binary compares 622 files clean. One divergence class is recorded in the archive README rather than fixed: on the malformed `parseterm_*` inputs, which postdate the freeze, both parsers reject with the same diagnostic at the same span but dump different partial trees. Rejected files never reach IR compare and the goldens assert diagnostics, not dumps, so no gate ever pinned that shape; dusk's recovery is the living behavior.

Bootstrapping without Rust.

- Every release tag now ships three artifacts: the linux x86_64 `dusk` binary, `dusk.ll.xz`, the compiler's own textual IR compressed, and a `sha256sums` whose IR hash equals the hash the release's stage ladder printed. A fresh machine either runs the binary, or, trusting only text, links the IR: `clang dusk.ll runtime/*.c -pthread -lm -o dusk`.
- `tools/bootstrap.sh` drives both paths: hand it the binary or the `.ll`/`.ll.xz` and it stands up a seed, self builds `compiler/dusk.dusk` with it under the usual resource cage, and prints the suite commands to verify the result, plus the no-artifact audit recipe. One reproducibility contract surfaced while proving it: a fault location string embeds the source path as spelled on the command line, and the library paths resolved through `DUSK_HOME` land in the same constants, so the recorded IR hash reproduces from the same checkout path with the root spelled absolute, which is how every script here builds. Normalizing the embedded paths so the hash travels across machines is recorded future work.
- `tools/ratchet.sh` is the release gate the ratchet rule has always implied, now a script: the previous release's binary must build the current compiler source, and the result must pass the full manifest. Run at every cut from here on.
- The AUR package builds from the seed artifact instead of cargo: `PKGBUILD` links the latest release's `dusk.ll.xz` into a seed with clang, self builds the tree, and installs `dusk`, `dawn`, and the share tree; `x86_64` only, since the IR pins that triple. Non linux bootstraps take the archive's tag walk.
- The docs follow: the README gains the bootstrap section and the archive pointer and loses the cargo story, `dawn.md` builds dawn from `compiler/dawn.dusk`, and the tsan, kqueue, dumps, and testing pages name the dusk commands.

Numbers.

- The archive stands alone: fresh `dusk-rust` clone, `cargo build --release` clean, `cargo test` fully green, 458 unit plus 596 golden plus 13 parser termination, differential parse over 622 shared corpus files clean.
- The ratchet holds: the `v1.3.0` release binary builds the 1.3.1 source and the freshly built compiler passes `testrun` 609/609.
- The stage ladder re-fixed on the renamed root with the `v1.3.0` release binary as seed: stage1, stage2, and stage3 share one binary sha256 (9045ee6e...) and one compiler IR sha256 (1c00139b...), the collapse, fixpoint, and determinism checks all green, the runner reporting 609/609 under stage1 and stage2 alike.
- The cold boot reproduces the release byte for byte: `tools/bootstrap.sh` links `dusk.ll.xz` against the runtime with clang, the seed self builds the source, and the result lands on the identical binary and IR hashes the ladder printed, with the suite green under the cold booted compiler.
- The audit path reproduces the release from the archive: `cargo build --release` in `dusk-rust`, that Rust compiler builds the `v1.3.0` compiler source there, and that binary builds this release's source here, landing on the identical binary and IR hashes, two hops from a Rust toolchain to the tip.
- The naive alternative, chaining binaries tag by tag from `v1.0.0`, was walked once and is recorded as broken, which is the walk doing its job: every hop compiles, the ratchet holding at each step, but the terminal compiler fails exactly one golden. Traced: `v1.0.x` shipped a lexer defect, a literal backslash directly before `u{` parsed as a unicode escape, fixed in 1.1.0, and a `v1.0.x` binary building the 1.1.0 compiler mis-reads the escape tables in that source, so the walk built lineage inherits the defect, an accidental trusting-trust propagation the releases themselves never saw, each having been built by the Rust compiler at its own tag under the lockstep. The ratchet rule guarantees the previous release builds the next source; it does not launder a semantic bug across many hops, so the audit path above seeds from the archive's Rust tree at the wanted tag instead of chaining.

## 1.3.0

The native harness. Every release through 1.2.0 checked itself with `cargo test`, a harness written in Rust driving a compiler written in dusk; 1.3.0 closes that gap from the other side, a golden test runner written in dusk itself, reading the same class of case out of a flat manifest instead of a hard coded Rust function. Alongside it, `dawn`, the package tool, gets a second implementation under `compiler/`, a dusk root beside the compiler's own, so the last major tool in the repository that was still Rust only now has a dusk twin that matches it line for line. No language surface changes; this release is entirely in test infrastructure, the package tool, and one small standard library addition underneath it.

A golden test runner, written in dusk.

- `testrun`, under `tests/runner/`, reads a manifest of flat text records and runs each one through a compiler you name, checking stdout, stderr, and exit code against what the record expects. `tests/runner/manifest.dusk` parses the format, `exec.dusk` shells the command and captures its streams, `compare.dusk` checks a record's expectation against what came back, `builtins.dusk` carries the one special that needs more than a single command, and `report.dusk` tallies and prints the run. The manifest format, the seven verdict modes (`run`, `run_raw`, `check_fail`, `check_ok`, `build_fail`, `tool`, `special`), and the full command line are specified in `docs/testing.md`.
- The manifest parser collects every error it can find, an unknown field, a bad escape, a duplicate test name among them, and reports all of them before the runner executes a single command, so a malformed manifest fails loudly instead of silently passing as an empty run.
- An exact match checks two things, not one: the captured bytes agree, and a second `wc -c` byte count agrees too, so a truncating NUL inside a captured stream can never read equal to a shorter expected value the way a plain string compare would.
- `err_absent` is the negative counterpart to the existing substring match, asserting a fragment must not appear in stderr, the shape a diagnostic reject that must name one cause and must not name another needs.
